His solution was to organise a semi-monthly "knowledge exchange" call between the department heads of 3 top universities to discuss the challenges around his topic of choice. Furthermore, it was set up as a conference call where any other universities could dial in and listen to the best practices of the big 3.

他的解决方案是：让三所顶尖大学的部门负责人每半个月召开一次“知识交流”会议，共同探讨与他所选主题相关的各种挑战。此外，该会议还向其他大学开放，其他大学可以加入会议，了解这三所顶尖大学的最佳实践。

By simply organising the call and playing host, he immediately absorbed all the credibility of the top universities and got direct phone access to a pile of great leads.

他只需负责安排通话并担任主持人而已，就能立刻获得那些顶尖大学的信任。此外，他还能直接与许多重要人物取得联系。

Every business is different. Don't just copy what someone else is doing. Consider your own situation, and then get clever.

每家企业的情况都不同。不要盲目模仿别人的做法。请先考虑自身的实际情况，然后再想办法解决问题。

打造温馨的片头画面/营造温馨的起始效果Creating warm intros

Warm intros are the goal. Conversations are infinitely easier when you get an intro through a mutual friend that establishes your credibility and reason for being there.

良好的开场白至关重要。如果通过共同的朋友来引荐彼此，那么对话就会容易得多。这样的引荐方式有助于确立双方的信任关系，也能让人明白彼此出现在这里的理由。

7 层培根夹心7 degrees of bacon

The world is a relatively small place. Everyone knows someone. We just have to remember to ask.

世界其实是个相当小的地方。每个人都能认识一些人。我们只需要记得去主动打听而已。

I was talking to a team of recent graduates who needed to reach McKinsey style consultants. They were pulling their hair out. We were in a coworking space full of other young entrepreneurs, so I just stood on a chair and yelled, “Excuse me! Does anyone here know anyone who works at McKinsey? Can we talk to you for a second? We’ll buy you a beer — you could really help us out!” They bought 3 beers, had 3 quick chats, and left with a diary full of intros.

我当时正在与一群刚毕业的年轻人交谈，他们希望能接触到像麦肯锡那样的咨询师。他们显得非常苦恼。我们当时身处一个充满其他年轻创业者的共享办公空间里。于是我站在椅子上大声喊道：“不好意思！这里有人认识在麦肯锡工作的人吗？能和我们聊聊吗？我们请你们喝啤酒——你们真的能帮到我们！”他们买了三瓶啤酒，我们简短地交谈了几句后，他们离开了，不过他们记下了不少有用的信息。

This is even easier for consumer products. Not everyone knows folks at McKinsey, but everybody does know, for example, a recent mom or amateur athlete or theatre enthusiast.

对于消费品来说，这一点更为容易理解。虽然不是每个人都知道麦肯锡的同事是谁，但大家都认识那些刚成为母亲的年轻女性、业余运动员或戏剧爱好者吧。

凯文·贝肯提出的“六度分隔理论”同样适用于与客户的交流。只要多次询问，你就能找到任何你想联系的人。

Kevin Bacon’s 7 degrees of separation applies to customer conversations. You can find anyone you need if you ask for it a couple times.

行业顾问Industry advisors

I relied heavily on advisors in my first company. We didn't know the industry and nobody took us seriously. Our 5 advisors each had around a half percent of equity and basically just made credible intros. I met with each once per month, so I ended up getting a fresh batch of intros weekly without it being a huge time burden for any of them.

在我创办的第一家公司里，我非常依赖各种顾问的建议。我们对这个行业一无所知，也没人把我们当回事。那 5 位顾问各自持有大约 0.5%的公司股份，他们的主要职责就是提供一些专业的分析意见而已。我每个月与每位顾问会面一次，这样我就能每周都能获得最新的分析意见，而这对他们来说也不会造成太大的时间负担。

On a bit of a tangent, you'd be surprised by the quality of the folks you can get to join your advisory board. The first conversation with a good advisor looks similar to the first conversation with a flagship customer: you get along and are talking about a space you both care about. You can sometimes poach killer advisors from your early customer conversations.

顺便说一下，你可能会惊讶于那些能够加入你顾问团的人的素质之高。与优秀的顾问进行初次交谈时，感觉就像与重要客户交谈一样：彼此相处融洽，谈论的都是双方都感兴趣的话题。有时候，你甚至可以从与早期客户的交流中发掘出优秀的顾问人选。

大学们Universities

I'm jealous of founders who are still in (or recently out of) university. Professors are a goldmine for intros. They get their grant funding from high-level industry folks they're on good terms with. And since they’re investing in research, those industry folks are self-selected to be excited about new projects.

我真羡慕那些仍在大学读书或刚从大学毕业的创业者。教授们可是获取各种信息的绝佳来源。他们能从与自己关系良好的行业大亨那里获得研究经费。而且，由于这些行业大亨本身就热衷于投资科研项目，因此他们自然会积极支持各种新项目。

Professors are easy to get in touch with if you don't know them yet. They post their emails and you can just wander into their office.

如果你还不认识各位教授，其实很容易与他们取得联系。他们都会把邮箱地址公布出来，你可以直接去他们的办公室找他们。

投资者Investors

Top-tier investors are awesome for B2B intros. Beyond their own rolodex and company portfolio, they can usually pull off cold intros to practically any industry. Investors can also help you close higher-tier advisors and directors than you'd be able to wrangle on your own.

顶级投资者在推动 B2B 业务方面的作用极为重要。除了他们自己的人脉网络和所投资的公司之外，他们通常还能帮助企业与几乎所有行业的潜在客户建立联系。此外，投资者还能协助你接触到那些单靠自己很难争取到的高层顾问和决策者。

Who has bought in to your idea already? Who could they connect you to?

已经有人接受你的想法了吗？他们能帮你联系到谁呢？

用于抵消债务的现金Cash in favours

Remember all those people who brushed you off by saying, “Sounds great, keep me in the loop and let me know how I can help.”

还记得那些对你说“听起来不错，随时告诉我进展，也需要我帮忙的话就告诉我”的人吧。

Now’s the time to call in those favours. Yes, they might not have actually meant it, but who cares? Reply back to that ancient email and tell them you’re ready for an intro to that guy they know. Use the format in the next section to make their lives easy and reassure them that you aren't going to waste anybody's time.

现在正是寻求他人帮助的好时机。没错，他们或许并非真心这么想，但谁在乎呢？回复那封旧邮件吧，告诉他们你已经准备好去认识他们所认识的那个人了。按照下一节中的格式来回复，这样就能让他们省心不少，同时也能让他们放心，你不会浪费任何人的时间。

You’ll get ignored a lot, but again, who cares? You aren’t trying to minimise your failure rate; you’re trying to get a few conversations going. The person you’re being introduced to won’t know the backstory anyway, so it’s a clean start from there.

你会经常被忽视，但谁在乎呢？你本来就不是为了降低失败率而这么做，而是想试着开启一些对话而已。反正，被你介绍给对方的人并不了解事情的来龙去脉，所以完全可以重新开始。

I wouldn’t make a habit of doing stuff like this since it’s a bit annoying and can burn bridges, but sometimes you’re backed into a corner and need to get started somehow.

我不会把做这种事当作一种习惯，因为这很烦人，还可能破坏人际关系。不过，有时候实在别无选择，只能勉强这么做。

提出会议请求并安排会议事宜Asking for and framing the meeting

Sometimes a proper meeting can't be avoided. For example, you might want the full hour or need to talk to someone senior outside of your peer or networking group. But since you don't necessarily have anything to sell, it's unclear what the meeting is for. In those cases, the right explanation and framing can do wonders.

有时候，召开正式会议是不可避免的。比如，你可能需要整整一小时的讨论时间，或者需要与同行或社交圈之外的高层人士进行沟通。但由于你并不一定有什么东西要推销，因此很难明确召开会议的真正目的。在这种情况下，恰当的解释和说明方式就能起到关键作用。

If you don’t know why you’re there, it becomes a sales meeting by default, which is bad for 3 reasons. First, the customer closes up about some important topics like pricing. Second, attention shifts to you instead of them. And finally, it’s going to be the worst sales meeting ever because you aren’t ready.

如果你不知道自己为何要去那里，那么这次会议本质上就变成了一次销售会议。而这样做有三个坏处：首先，客户会回避一些重要的话题，比如价格问题。其次，大家的注意力会集中在你身上，而不是客户身上。最后，因为你们没有做好准备，这次会议肯定会变成最糟糕的销售会议。

Symptoms:

症状：

  • “Um. So….”“嗯……那么……”
  • “How’s it going?”“怎么样了？”

There are a lot of bad ways to frame the meeting, both when first asking for it and once it begins.

在提议召开会议或会议进行过程中，都有很多不恰当的安排方式。

Questions like, "Can I interview you" or "Thanks for agreeing to this interview" both set set off alarm bells that this meeting is going to be super boring. I don't want to be interviewed; I want to talk and help!

像“我可以采访您吗”或“感谢您同意接受采访”这样的提问，都让人觉得这次会面肯定会非常无聊。我不想接受采访；我想要的是进行交流、提供帮助！

The common, "Can I get your opinion on what we're doing?" sets expectations of neediness and that you want compliments or approval.

那种常见的提问方式，比如“请问您对我们正在做的事情有什么看法？”，会让人觉得提问者缺乏自信，或者希望得到别人的赞美和认可。

No expectations at all are set by, "Do you have time for a quick coffee/lunch/chat/meeting?" which suggests you're liable to waste my time.

“你有时间一起喝杯咖啡/吃顿饭/聊聊天/开个会吗？”这样的邀请根本不值得期待，因为这显然意味着你会浪费我的时间。

The framing format I like has 5 key elements.

我喜欢的框架结构包含 5 个关键要素。

  1. You're an entrepreneur trying to solve horrible problem X, usher in wonderful vision Y, or fix stagnant industry Z. Don't mention your idea.你是一名企业家，试图解决棘手的难题 X、实现美好的愿景 Y，或扭转停滞不前的行业 Z 的现状。请不要提及你的具体想法。
  2. Frame expectations by mentioning what stage you're at and, if it's true, that you don't have anything to sell.在阐述自己的期望时，先说明自己目前处于哪个阶段。如果属实的话，也要坦白表示自己目前没有任何东西可以出售。
  3. Show weakness and give them a chance to help by mentioning your specific problem that you're looking for answers on. This will also clarify that you're not a time waster.表现出自己的弱点，让他们有机会提供帮助。具体说明自己需要解决的难题。这样也能表明你不会浪费别人的时间。
  4. Put them on a pedestal by showing how much they, in particular, can help.通过展现他们各自能做出的贡献，来凸显他们的价值。/通过强调每个人所能发挥的作用，来抬高他们的地位。
  5. Ask for help.寻求帮助吧。

Or, in shorter form: Vision / Framing / Weakness / Pedestal / Ask

或者，简而言之：愿景/框架/弱点/基础/问题/需求

The mnemonic is “Very Few Wizards Properly Ask [for help]." Here's what it might look like before you have a product:

这个助记口诀是：“很少有巫师会真正寻求帮助。”在产品尚未问世之前，情况大概就是如此。

Hey Pete,

嘿，皮特，

I'm trying to make desk & office rental less of a pain for new businesses (vision). We're just starting out and don't have anything to sell, but want to make sure we're building something that actually helps (framing).

我的目标是让新创业企业在租赁办公桌椅和办公空间时能少些麻烦。我们才刚刚起步，目前还没有什么产品可以销售，但希望自己能够创造出真正有用的产品/服务来帮助这些企业。

I've only ever come at it from the tenant's side and I'm having a hard time understanding how it all works from the landlord's perspective (weakness). You've been renting out desks for a while and could really help me cut through the fog (pedestal).

我一直都是从租户的角度来思考这个问题，很难理解房东那边是怎么看的（这是我的弱点）。你已经从事办公桌出租业务有一段时间了，一定能帮我弄清楚其中的门道。

Do you have time in the next couple weeks to meet up for a chat? (ask)

在接下来的几周里，你有时间见面聊聊吗？（询问）

Sometimes the 5 parts will be combined into just one or two sentences, or they can be in a different order. For example, the next email sounded a little too pitchy and I was worried he would delete it as sales spam before getting through the first two sentences. As such, I moved my admission of weakness as early as I could get it:

有时候，这五个部分会被合并成一两句话来表达，或者它们的顺序也会被调整。比如，下一封邮件的语气显得有些急切了，我担心他在读完前两句话之前就会将其视为垃圾邮件而删除。因此，我把承认自己有缺点的部分提前放在了邮件中：

Hey Scott, I run a startup trying to make advertising more playful and ultimately effective (vision).

嘿，斯科特，我经营着一家初创公司，致力于让广告变得更有趣、更有效果（这就是我们的愿景）。

We're having a load of trouble figuring out how all the pieces of the industry fit together and where we can best fit into it (weakness). You know more about this industry than anyone and could really save us from a ton of mistakes (pedestal).

我们在理解该行业的各个组成部分如何相互关联、以及我们应在其中扮演什么角色方面遇到了很大困难（弱点）。您对该行业的了解远远超过其他人，一定能帮助我们避免犯下许多错误（优势）。

We're funded and have a couple products out already, but this is in no way a sales meeting -- we're just moving into a new area and could really use some of your expertise (framing).

我们有足够的资金支持，也已经有几款产品上市了。不过，这次并非是为了推销产品——我们只是要进入一个新的领域，因此非常需要你们在专业方面的帮助。

Can you spare a bit of time in the next week to help point us in the right direction over a coffee? (ask)

下周您能抽出一点时间来吗？我们可以边喝咖啡边聊聊，帮我们指明正确的方向。（请求）

People like to help entrepreneurs. But they also hate wasting their time. An opening like this tells them that you know what you need and that they'll be able to make a real difference.

人们愿意帮助创业者。但他们也讨厌浪费时间。这样的机会让他们觉得：你清楚自己需要什么，而他们也能真正发挥作用、做出贡献。

Once the meeting starts, you have to grab the reins or it's liable to turn into them drilling you on your idea, which is exactly what you don't want. To do this, I basically repeat what I said in the email and then immediately drop into the first question. If someone else made the introduction, I'll use them as a voice of authority:

一旦会议开始，你就必须掌控局面，否则对方很可能会一直对你的想法指手画脚，而这正是你最不想看到的。为此，我通常会重复一下在邮件里说过的内容，然后立即转入第一个问题的讨论。如果是由其他人来作介绍的话，我会利用他的话作为权威意见来支持自己的观点。

Hey Tim, thanks so much for taking the time.

嗨，蒂姆，非常感谢你抽出时间来。

As I mentioned in the email, we're trying to make it easier for universities to spin out student businesses (vision) and aren't exactly sure how it all works yet (framing & weakness).

正如我在邮件中提到的，我们希望让大学能够更轻松地推动学生创业项目的发展。不过，我们还不太清楚具体的运作方式是什么（在框架构建和实施细节方面还存在不足）。

I think Tom made this intro (authority) because you have pretty unique insight into what's going on behind the curtain and could really help us get pointed in the right direction (pedestal)… (introductions continue)

我觉得是汤姆做了这个介绍。因为他对于幕后发生的事情有着独到的见解，肯定能帮助我们找到正确的方向……（介绍继续中）

I was looking at your spinout portfolio and it's pretty impressive, especially company X. How did they get from your classroom to where they are now? (grab the reins and ask good questions)

我看了你们旗下的子公司们，真是令人印象深刻，尤其是 X 公司。它们是从你们的实验室发展成现在的规模的，究竟经历了怎样的过程呢？（主动提问，深入了解）

These conversations are easy to screw up. As such, you need to be the one in control. You set the agenda, you keep it on topic, and you propose next steps. Don't be a jerk about it, but do have a plan for the meeting and be assertive about keeping it on track.

这类对话很容易搞砸。因此，你必须掌控局面。由你来确定讨论的主题、确保谈话不偏离正轨，并决定接下来的步骤。不要表现得无礼粗暴，但一定要为会议做好计划，确保一切按计划进行。

It’s worth noting that this is how I set up meetings from warm intros. The main goal is to clarify what I need and how they can help. Cold approaches are a totally different beast and are much more of a gamble. Again, the point of cold calls is to stop having them. Find a clever ways to generate warm intros and tell those people how they can help. You’ll have a much easier time.

值得注意的是，我都是通过这种方式来开启会议的：先进行友好的寒暄。这样做的目的是明确自己的需求以及对方能提供怎样的帮助。而那种毫无铺垫的直接联系方式则完全不同，风险也更大。再说一次，与其采用那种直接联系的方式，不如想办法建立良好的沟通关系，然后再告诉对方自己需要什么帮助。这样会容易得多。

6.2是通勤还是打电话呢？To commute or to call

One of the solutions to the time cost of these conversations is to move them onto Skype or phone calls. In most situations, I don’t think the added volume is worth the information you lose by not being in the room. I get confused enough by what people are telling me in person. Losing access to their face and body language feels like shooting myself in the foot.

解决这些通话所耗费的时间问题的方法之一，就是把通话转移到 Skype 或电话上进行。在大多数情况下，我觉得，为了节省一点时间而失去当面交流时所能获取的信息，实在不值得。人们当面说话时，我的理解能力已经够差了；如果再无法看到对方的表情和肢体语言，那就相当于自讨苦吃。

More subtly, calls damage the delicate power dynamic of these conversations. When someone is having a coffee with you, there’s the potential to chat as friends. You can just shoot the breeze about the industry for a bit. You can keep it casual. They’re enjoying the conversation

更微妙的是，打电话会破坏这种对话中微妙的权力平衡。当有人与你一起喝咖啡时，你们可以像朋友一样聊天。你可以随便聊聊这个行业的相关话题。气氛可以很轻松。双方都能享受这样的对话。

The same is decidedly not true on the phone or Skype. People try to squeeze calls in between other activities, wondering how quickly they can “finish with business” and hang up. Folks on the phone are super annoyed when you “just want to chat”. So you need to make the whole thing more formal, which is one of the exact mistakes we’re trying to avoid!

但在电话或 Skype 通话中，情况显然并非如此。人们总是试图在其他事务的间隙里打打电话，希望尽快“办完正事”然后挂断电话。如果你只是想闲聊，那肯定会让通话对方非常恼火。因此，你必须让整个通话过程显得更正式一些——而这恰恰是我们想要避免的错误做法！

Phone calls end up sounding more like scripted interviews than natural conversations, because they are. It’s a constraint of the medium.

电话交谈听起来更像是按照事先写好的脚本进行的对话，而非自然的交流。因为这就是这种交流方式的固有局限。

Plus, nobody becomes friends over the phone, which means you aren't going to get the warm intros and future meetings you need. Setting up a call feels like less overhead in the short-term, but that’s because you aren’t yet able to see all the time-saving and business-saving benefits of in-person relationships with your customers.

此外，没人会通过电话成为朋友。这意味着你无法获得那些有助于建立良好关系的初次交流机会，也无法安排后续的会面。虽然短期内通过电话沟通似乎更省事，但那是因为你还没有意识到与客户面对面交流所带来的各种节省时间和成本的好处。

That being said, some other great people in the field like and recommend phone calls. Use whatever works. But I will say that you should start in person. It’s too easy to use surveys or phone calls as an excuse to skip the awkwardness of meeting in person rather than as a considered trade-off.

话虽如此，该领域里还有一些杰出人士更倾向于使用电话沟通的方式。只要哪种方式更合适，那就用哪种吧。不过我还是要说，最好还是从当面交流开始。把问卷调查或电话沟通当作逃避面对面交流的借口，这种做法太容易犯了。与其如此，不如把它们当作一种必要的妥协方式来接受。

6.3这种建议性的转变/这种建议性的调整The advisory flip

In terms of mindset, don't go into these discussions looking for customers. It creates a needy vibe and forfeits the position of power. Instead, go in search of industry and customer advisors. You are just trying to find helpful, knowledgable people who are excited about your idea.

在心态上，不要以寻找客户的姿态来参与这些讨论。那样会给人一种迫切需要成交的印象，从而失去主动权。相反，你应该寻找行业专家和客户顾问。你只是想找到那些乐于助人、知识渊博、并且对你所提出的想法感兴趣的人而已。

With that mindset switch, you'll know why you're there. And instead of it being a customer-learning-but-I-really-want-to-do-sales meeting, it's a let-me-find-out-if-you-are-a-good-advisor-by-asking-lots-of-questions meeting.

有了这种思维方式的转变，你就会明白自己为何身处那里。这样一来，这次会议就不再是“客户在学习，而我则急于推销产品”的场合，而变成了“通过提出大量问题来判断对方是否是合适的顾问”的过程。

Willpower is a finite resource. The way to overcome difficult situations isn't to power through, but rather to change your circumstances to require less willpower. Changing the context of the meeting to "looking for advisors" is the equivalent of throwing out all your chocolate when you start a diet. You change the environment to naturally facilitate your goals.

意志力是一种有限的资源。应对困难情况的正确方式，不是强行硬撑，而是改变环境，从而减少对意志力的需求。把会议的性质改为“寻求建议”，就好比在开始节食时把所有的巧克力都扔掉一样。通过改变环境，就能更自然地实现自己的目标。

You don't need to explicitly tell them you're looking for advisors. In fact, I wouldn't unless you already quite like them and it happens to come up in conversation. It's really about orienting your state of mind to give you a helpful internal narrative and consistent front.

你不必明确告诉他们你正在寻找顾问。事实上，除非你真的喜欢他们，而且这个话题在谈话中自然而然地被提及，否则没必要特意说出来。关键在于调整自己的心态，从而形成有助于自己决策的思维方式，同时保持一致的对外形象。

Somewhat counterintuitively, the sales-advisor switch also puts you firmly in control of the meeting, since you're now evaluating them. Even if the topics of discussion are basically the same, you (and they) will notice the difference.

有点反直觉的是，由销售顾问来主导会议其实反而能让你更好地掌控整个会议进程，因为你现在是在评估他们的工作表现。即便讨论的主题大致相同，你和销售顾问都会察觉到其中的差别。

6.4开了多少次会议How many meetings

Every meeting has an opportunity cost. While you’re sitting in a bus on your way there, you aren’t writing code or generating leads or drinking wine.

每次开会都有机会成本。当你坐在公交车上前往会议地点时，你就无法编写代码、开发新客户或品酒了。

If you’re running a sales-driven business (especially enterprise sales), the opportunity cost of early conversations is low since many of them will become sales leads. You’re doubling up on learning and dealflow.

如果你经营的是以销售为导向的业务（尤其是企业销售领域），那么在早期与潜在客户沟通所付出的机会成本其实很低，因为其中很多潜在客户最终都会转化为销售机会。这样一来，你既能够获取有价值的信息，又能推动销售进程。

The UX community (who knows their customer conversation!) says you should keep talking to people until you stop hearing new information.

用户体验领域的专家们认为：你应该一直与人们交流，直到再也听不到任何新的信息为止。

In my experience, that could take as few as 3-5 conversations if you have a relatively simple industry and a focused customer segment. If you’ve run more than 10 conversations and are still getting results that are all over the map, then I’d guess that your customer segment is too fuzzy and could stand to be tightened up.

以我的经验来看，如果你所处的行业比较简单，目标客户群体也较为明确，那么通过 3 到 5 次沟通就足以取得成效。如果你已经进行了 10 次以上的沟通，但得到的结果仍然参差不齐，那我觉得你的目标客户群体定义得不够清晰，需要进一步明确才行。

This isn’t about having a thousand meetings. It’s about quickly learning what you need, and then getting back to building your business. You should be able to answer almost any customer question and move onto new ones within a week.

这并不是要开上千次会议。关键在于迅速了解自己所需的信息，然后继续专注于业务的发展。你应该能够在一周之内回答几乎所有客户的疑问，并着手处理新的任务。

持续交谈下去，直到听不到任何新信息为止。

Keep having conversations until you stop hearing new stuff.

7选择你的客户CHAPTER SEVEN · Choosing your customers

They say that startups don’t starve, they drown. You never have too few options, too few leads, or too few ideas. You have too many. You get overwhelmed. You do a little bit of everything.

人们常说，初创企业不是因为资源不足而倒闭，而是因为不堪重负而失败。其实，永远不可能出现选项太少、潜在客户太少或创意太少的情况。相反，往往都是反过来的——选项太多、潜在客户太多、创意太多。这就让人应接不暇。于是，只能什么都尝试一点。

When it comes to avoiding drowning and making faster progress, good customer segmentation is your best friend.

要想避免溺水风险并更快取得进展，合理的客户细分策略绝对是你的好帮手。

分段/分割Segmentation

When we look at the big successes, they seem to serve the whole world. Google lets anyone find anything. Paypal helps anyone send money anywhere. Evernote backs up all the writing of everybody.

当我们看到那些巨大的成功案例时，会发现它们似乎都惠及了整个世界。谷歌让任何人都能轻松找到自己想要的信息。贝宝则帮助人们把钱汇送到世界的任何角落。Evernote 则负责备份每个人的全部文字记录。

But they didn’t start there. If you start too generic, everything is watered down. Your marketing message is generic. You suffer feature creep. Google helped PhD students find obscure bits of code. EBay helped collectors buy and sell Pez dispensers. Evernote helped moms save and share recipes.

但他们并非从那里开始起步的。如果起点过于宽泛，那么一切都会变得毫无重点。营销信息也会变得千篇一律。谷歌帮助博士生寻找那些难以找到的代码片段。eBay 则帮助收藏家买卖 Pez 糖果机。Evernote 则帮助母亲们保存和分享食谱。

When you have a fuzzy sense of who you’re serving, you end up talking to a lot of different types of people, which leads to confusing signals and three problems:

当你不清楚自己究竟要为谁服务时，你就会不得不与各种各样的人打交道。这会导致信息混乱，进而引发三个问题：

  1. You get overwhelmed by options and don’t know where to start面对众多的选择，你不知该从何入手。
  2. You aren’t moving forward but can’t prove yourself wrong你既无法取得进步，又无法证明自己是错的。
  1. You receive incredibly mixed feedback and can’t make sense of it你收到的反馈褒贬不一，让人难以理解。

是想要宝宝，还是想成为健美运动员呢？Babies or body builders?

I talked to a woman who had developed a very cool powdered condiment. It was sweet (like a cinnamon brown sugar), but had all the nutrition of a multivitamin. In fact, it was an all-natural superfood — you could eat nothing but that powder and survive indefinitely.

我与一位女士交谈过，她发明了一种非常棒的粉状调味品。这种调味品带有甜味（类似肉桂和红糖的味道），同时还含有多种维生素所具备的营养成分。实际上，这是一种完全天然的超级食品——只要食用这种调味粉，就无需再吃别的东西，也能维持生命。

She said that it had countless uses: you could sprinkle it on your breakfast or mix it in with your protein shake. Moms could trick their kids into being healthy. Restaurants could leave it on the tables as a healthy sugar alternative. However, she was running in circles because the bodybuilders wanted one thing, the restaurants wanted another, and the moms needed a third. Making one of them happy always disappointed the others. She didn’t know how to start.

她说，这种物质有着无数的用途：可以撒在早餐上，也可以混入蛋白质奶昔中。妈妈们可以用它来让孩子更健康地进食。餐厅则可以把它作为健康的糖分替代品摆在上菜桌上。不过，她觉得事情很复杂：健美运动员想要一种用途，餐厅想要另一种用途，而妈妈们则又有不同的需求。试图让其中一方满意，往往会让其他方失望。她不知道该从何入手。

Before we can serve everyone, we have to serve someone. By thinking about who would be most likely to buy, she realised it was moms with young kids who are currently shopping at health food stores. She also knew where to find them: in the health food stores! But the stores were an important partner and she didn’t know what they would want.

在能够为所有人提供服务之前，我们必须先为某个人提供服务。经过思考，她意识到，最有可能购买这些产品的，应该是那些带着年幼孩子的母亲们。她也知道该去哪里找到她们：当然就是在健康食品店里！不过，这些健康食品店也是重要的合作伙伴，而她并不清楚他们到底想要什么。

She decided to kill two birds with one stone by going into only the small, independent health food stores (slice that segment!) and asking them to place a few bottles of her condiment beside the breakfast foods. It’s a great way to cut through opinions by asking for a commitment (some shelf space) and she would come back in a week to ask them about their experiences. If the stores were happy and the product sold, she could begin finding ways to talk to their customers (and her end consumers), perhaps via an in-store tasting.

她决定一石二鸟：只选择那些规模较小、独立经营的保健食品店，要求他们在早餐食品旁边摆放几瓶自己的调味品。通过这种方式，她能够确保自己的产品能被顾客看到。一周后，她会再回去了解这些店的销售情况。如果这些店愿意继续销售她的产品，她还会想办法与这些店的顾客——也就是最终消费者——取得联系，比如在店里举办产品品尝会。

By getting specific, she was able to cut through the noise, stop drowning, and start making progress.

通过具体化处理，她能够摆脱各种干扰，不再陷入混乱状态，从而开始取得进步。

大品牌还是小店？Big brands or mom & pop?

I was once thrilled that my customer segment was “advertisers”. Everyone advertises somehow, so naturally this was very exciting. Our market was practically infinite! I talked to mom-and-pop shops, e-tailers, big brands, creative agencies, SMEs, music labels and more. Everyone I talked to had different needs, constraints, problems, and desires.

我曾经很高兴，因为我的客户群体正是“广告主”。毕竟，每个人都需要做广告，所以这当然让我觉得非常兴奋。我们的市场几乎是无限的！我和各种类型的客户打过交道：小店主、电商企业、大型品牌、广告公司、中小企业、音乐唱片公司等等。每个客户都有不同的需求、限制、问题和期望。

Everything we did ended up sort of working. Everything was somewhat promising. Some people were talking about paying us $10,000/month while others scoffed at the idea of paying $10. Every feature was more or less well-received. But if we tried to cut any of those middling features, we would get an uproar. It was always someone’s favourite part.

我们所做的每一件事似乎都取得了不错的效果。一切看起来都挺有希望的。有些人提议每月支付我们 10,000 美元，而另一些人则对支付 10 美元这样的提议嗤之以鼻。每一个功能都得到了不错的反响。不过，如果我们试图去掉其中任何一个“一般般”的功能，都会引发轩然大波。因为那个功能往往是某个人的“最爱”。

In reality, our customer segment was just too broad. We were trying to serve everyone simultaneously. We said “yes” to every request. Every debate over a new feature could be won by claiming, “Well, those guys would love it.” The reverse argument could be made to prevent any feature’s removal.

实际上，我们的客户群体过于广泛了。我们试图同时满足所有人的需求。对于每一个请求，我们都选择同意。在决定是否添加新功能时，我们总是以“那些人肯定会喜欢这个功能”作为理由来支持添加该功能；而反对删除某个功能时，则会用“大家都会喜欢这个功能”来作为反对理由。

The big trouble was that we could prove ourselves neither right nor wrong. We could never say that a new idea had really worked or totally failed. We were paying attention to so many customer segments that there was pretty much always someone it worked for. But making a so-so product for a bunch of audiences isn’t quite the same as making an incredible product for one.

最大的问题在于，我们无法确定自己的想法是对是错。我们永远无法确定某个新想法究竟是否真的有效或彻底失败了。由于我们需要兼顾如此多的客户群体，因此总会有某些客户觉得该产品不错。不过，为众多客户打造一个“还行”的产品，与为某个特定客户打造一款出色的产品，显然是两回事。

Getting specific about who your ideal customers are allows you to filter out all the noise which comes from everyone else. In our case, we eventually noticed unusually strong signals from creative agencies who wanted to be edgy. We cut a bunch of features and were finally able to get a sense of what was working and what wasn’t.

明确了解自己的理想客户是谁，有助于排除来自其他方面的各种干扰。在我们的案例中，我们注意到那些希望打造独特风格的创意机构发出了强烈的信号。我们取消了一些功能后，终于弄清楚了哪些做法有效，哪些无效。

但这到底是什么意思呢！？But what does it mean!?

I recently spoke to a couple founders who have been working hard on their customer conversations. They were doing a great job of ensuring they use every sales meeting as an opportunity to learn. They’re asking good questions which pass The Mom Test and are pushing for advancement and commitment whenever it’s relevant. And yet, they have no idea what is going on and aren’t sure how to move the business to the next level. They don’t know which features to build and which to cut. They don’t know how to improve their marketing language. Why aren’t customer conversations helping them?

我最近与几位创业者进行了交谈，他们一直在努力与客户进行有效的沟通。他们很好地利用了每一次销售机会来获取有用信息。他们会提出一些经过深思熟虑的问题，同时也会在适当的时候推动业务的进一步发展。然而，他们却不清楚当前的情况，也不明白该如何将业务提升到新的水平。他们不知道该开发哪些功能，又该放弃哪些功能。他们也不懂得如何改进自己的营销语言。为什么与客户的沟通并没有给他们带来帮助呢？

To make matters worse, the feedback they’re getting is absurdly inconsistent. If they run twenty conversations, they end up with twenty different must-have features and twenty separate must-solve problems. The more people they talk to, the more confused they get. What’s going on here?

更糟糕的是，他们所收到的反馈也毫无逻辑可言。如果进行二十次沟通，最终得到的结果就是二十个不同的“必须实现的功能”和二十个需要解决的“问题”。与的人交谈越多，他们就越困惑。这到底是怎么回事呢？

Their customer segment was incredibly broad, but in a sneaky way. Imagine I tell you my customer segment is “students”. Okay, you say, with a picture of an American undergraduate university student in your head. Maybe it’s a male student. He sits down in the lecture hall, cracks open his mac (adding to the sea of glowing apples the professor’s view has recently become), and fires up reddit to help him survive the next ninety minutes.

他们的客户群体极其广泛，不过这种广泛性其实是一种“隐蔽”的方式。想象一下，如果我说我的客户群体是“学生”，你脑海中会浮现出什么样的人呢？也许会是一个美国大学生。他坐在教室里，打开笔记本电脑（这样一来，教授的视野里就又多了无数亮着的苹果图标），然后登录 Reddit 网站，好让自己能顺利度过接下来的 90 分钟。

So I’ve built a product for students, and feedback starts coming in. But it’s not what I expect. One user needs to add formal citations. Another wants practice questions. A third needs it to run on the iPad. A fourth needs eighty pupils to be able to use it on the same computer. The next needs to use the app through an intermittent internet connection. We’re looking at this list of requirements and our soul feels like it’s being forced through a colander. This will literally take us years to build. Where do we start?

所以我为学生们开发了一个产品，现在开始收到用户的反馈。但这些反馈并不如我所预期的那样。有个用户希望添加正式的引用格式；另一个用户想要练习题；还有一个人希望该产品在 iPad 上也能使用。还有用户要求至少有 80 名学生能够同时使用同一台电脑来使用该产品。另一些人则希望即使网络连接不稳定，也能正常使用该应用。看着这些要求，我们简直觉得心都要碎了。要完成这个产品，恐怕需要好几年的时间。我们该从哪里开始呢？

It turns out that “students” is a broader segment than we initially expected. The first is a PhD student. The second is an ambitious youngling at a prep school. The third is a homeschooling parent who wants to use it with her kid. The fourth is a rural village in the Indian rice belt where all the local kids are self-educating through the one village computer. The fifth is in Africa, running the app off a shaky cellphone connection. All are “students”.

原来，“学生”这个群体的范围比我们最初想象的要广泛得多。第一类是博士生。第二类是那些在预科学校学习的雄心勃勃的年轻人。第三类则是那些希望在家教育自己孩子的家长。第四类则位于印度水稻种植区的一个农村地区，那里的所有孩子都通过村里的唯一一台电脑来自学。第五类人在非洲，他们依靠不稳定的手机网络来使用这款应用程序。他们都属于“学生”范畴。

The founders I mentioned were having the same experience, but for “sales organisations” instead of “students”. There are countless different kinds of sales organisations with fundamentally different needs and workflows and tools and goals. But from the outside, they all look like companies who do sales. Even if you narrow it down with a demographic constraint, as these guys did (sales organisations with 25-250 salespeople), you’re still facing unfathomable diversity.

我提到的那些创业者也面临着同样的情况，只不过他们所面对的是“销售机构”，而不是“学生”。销售机构有无数种类型，它们在需求、工作流程、所使用的工具以及目标方面都有很大的差异。但从外部来看，它们都像是从事销售业务的公司而已。即便你试图通过某些条件来缩小范围，比如只考虑那些拥有 25 到 250 名销售人员的销售机构，你仍然会面临难以想象的多样性。

They weren’t having 20 conversations with their customers. They were having one conversation each with 20 different types of customers. That’s why the feedback was so inconsistent. It was as if they were exploring two dozen business ideas simultaneously.

他们并没有与每位客户进行 20 次交谈。实际上，他们只是与 20 种不同类型的客户分别进行了一次交谈而已。这就是为什么反馈如此不一致的原因。就好像他们在同时尝试着二十多种不同的商业模式一样。

In these cases, talking to an industry expert can be hugely informative to provide you with a taxonomy of the industry. That will give you a better starting point for choosing where to begin. If you don’t have access to this high-level view, you still want to take your best guess and get specific. The next section on Customer Slicing will show you one way to approach it. Over time, your understanding of the industry will improve and you can adjust your mental categories as needed. When the feedback is all over the map, it’s really hard to extract value. Once you get specific, you can learn.

在这种情况下，与行业专家交谈会非常有帮助，他们能帮你了解该行业的结构与分类。这会为你确定研究起点提供有力指导。即便无法获得这种宏观层面的信息，你也应尽力做出合理的推测并明确具体细节。下一节关于“客户细分”的内容会为你提供具体的处理方法。随着时间的推移，你对行业的理解会越来越深入，届时你可以根据需要调整自己的分类方式。当各种反馈信息杂乱无章时，很难从中提炼出有用的信息。但一旦你明确了具体细节，就能从中学习到有用的东西了。

如果你无法确定那些反复出现的问题和需求，那就说明你还没有明确界定出目标客户群体。

If you aren’t finding consistent problems and goals, you don’t yet have a specific enough customer segment.

客户细分/客户分类Customer slicing

If you’ve already got a product and users, you can explore your way to a good segment by seeing who likes it and focusing on that group. The other approach is to just make a best-guess. In both cases, you’re ending up with a specific idea of who you’re serving so you can really make the features and language resonate for them.

如果你已经拥有产品和服务，用户群体也确定下来了，那么你可以先了解哪些人喜欢你的产品，然后专注于服务这些用户。另一种方法则是凭直觉来决定目标用户群体。无论哪种方式，你都需要明确自己要服务的人群，这样才能确保产品的功能和表达方式能够真正符合他们的需求。

You’ll want even more specific segmentation for your conversations than for your whole business. Going out and talking to people is that it takes time. If you talk to five people who each have different goals, you’re going to get mixed signals which don’t give you the confidence you need to move forward.

与整个业务领域相比，你在对话中更需要进行更细致的区分和分类。与人们进行交流需要时间。如果你与五个目标各不相同的人交谈，你得到的信息就会混乱不堪，从而无法让你有信心继续前进。

This drilling down into ever more specific groups is called Customer Slicing. You take a segment and then keep slicing off better and better sub-sets of it until you’ve got a tangible sense of who you can go talk to and where you can find them. Start with a broad segment and ask:

这种将客户进一步细分为更具体的群体的做法，被称为“客户细分”。首先确定一个大的客户群体，然后不断从中划分出更小的子群体，直到能够明确知道应该与谁沟通、在何处可以找到他们。从大的客户群体开始，逐步进行细分吧。

  • Within this group, which type of this person would want it most?在这个群体中，哪种类型的人最想要这个呢？
  • Would everyone within this group buy/use it, or only some of them?这个群体中的每个人都会购买/使用它吗？还是只有部分人会使用呢？
  • Why do they want it? (e.g. What is their problem or goal)他们为什么想要它呢？（比如：他们面临什么问题或有什么目标呢？）
  • Does everyone in the group have that motivation or only some of them?这个团队里的每个人都有这样的动力吗？还是只有部分人而已？
  • What additional motivations are there?还有哪些额外的动机呢？
  • Which other types of people have these motivations?还有哪些类型的人会有这样的动机呢？

We now have a two groups of segments: the first is a collection of quite specific demographic groups and the second is a series of motivations. When we look at them, some will be more generic than others. Go back through the generic ones and keep slicing. Just repeat the questions above. Within that sub-group, who wants it most? Next we’re going to look at our groups’ behaviours and figure out where to find them.

我们现在有两组分类标准：第一组是各种特定的人群特征；第二组则是人们的各种动机。在分析这些标准时，有些标准比较通用，而有些则比较具体。对于那些比较通用的标准，我们可以继续深入分析。重复上述步骤即可。在每个子群体中，谁最想要这个东西呢？接下来，我们将研究各个群体的行为模式，从而确定他们在哪里。

  • What are these people already doing to achieve their goal or survive their problem?这些人为了实现自己的目标或解决所面临的问题，都在做些什么呢？
  • Where can we find our demographic groups?我们在哪里能够找到目标人群呢？
  • Where can we find people doing the above workaround behaviours?我们在哪里能找到那些采取上述变通措施的人呢？

Are any of these groups un-findable? If so, go back up the list and slice them into finer pieces until you know where to find them. A customer segment isn’t very useful if there’s no way you can get in touch!

这些群体中，有没有是无法找到的呢？如果有，那就重新查看列表，将它们进一步细分，直到找到对应的群体为止。如果无法与某个客户群体取得联系，那这个客户群体就毫无用处了！

Now that we have a bunch of who-where pairs, we can decide who to start with based on who seems most:

现在我们有了许多“谁在哪儿”的组合，我们可以根据谁看起来最合适来决定先从谁开始处理。

  1. Profitable盈利的/有利润的
  2. Easy to reach容易到达
  3. Rewarding for us to build a business around围绕这一点来开展业务，对我们来说很有意义。

For example, if we were thinking about building an app to help students become more confident speakers, we might decide the best-case-students are graduating students nervous about their first big job interview. They are motivated not to screw up their big day. Other folks with those motivations might be first-time TV/radio guests, someone giving a wedding speech, or new authors who are about to go on book tour. Additional motivations (beyond coping with nervousness about a particular event) might be to improve at a valuable long-term skill or to fix some of the quirks of being a non-native speaker. That gives us some additional demographics, such as young professionals who have to talk a lot, such as teachers and salespeople. Or foreign exchange students who are about to graduate. Or non-native speaking PhD students who have an upcoming conference talk.

例如，如果我们想开发一款帮助人们提高演讲能力的应用程序，那么最合适的用户群体可能是那些即将毕业、对第一次重要面试感到紧张的学生。他们有动力避免在关键时刻犯错。其他有类似需求的用户可能包括第一次在电视或广播上发言的人、需要发表婚礼演讲的人，以及即将开始图书推广的新作家。除了应对特定场合的紧张情绪之外，人们使用该应用的动机还可能包括提升某项长期有用的技能，或是克服作为非母语者所面临的种种困难。这样一来，我们就能确定更多目标用户群体了：比如需要经常演讲的年轻专业人士、教师和销售人员；即将毕业的外国交换生；以及即将参加学术会议的非母语者博士生。

As we get specific, it becomes easier to imagine where to find these groups. Instead of the generic “students” (I guess I’ll walk around campus?), we can easily find non-native speaking PhD students through the admissions office or the department advisors. We know exactly who to look for and won’t waste our time talking to a bunch of irrelevant folks. Or we can find people online. We might reach new authors by searching Amazon’s upcoming release list and then reaching out to them on Twitter.

一旦我们明确了目标群体，就更容易找到他们了。不必笼统地称其为“学生”（也许我可以去校园里四处问问吧？），我们可以通过招生办公室或各系的导师来找到那些不会说母语的博士生。这样，我们就知道该找谁了，不必浪费时间与那些无关的人交谈。或者，我们也可以在网上寻找这些人。比如，可以通过查看亚马逊上的新书列表，然后通过推特与这些作者取得联系。

Now that we’ve got some focused demographics, we also want to look at existing behaviours. A lot of people are terrified of speaking and just ignore it. They probably aren’t going to be our customer. Others talk to a therapist about their anxiety in general, while others try to improve speaking in particular by googling for tips, reading books, or going to workshops and meetups like Toastmasters. There are more existing solutions (e.g. new salespeople might get on-the-job training), but you get the idea.

既然我们已经明确了目标人群的特征，接下来还想了解一下这些人的行为习惯。很多人害怕公开演讲，因此选择不去面对这个问题。这些人很可能不会成为我们的客户。另一些人则会向心理治疗师倾诉自己的焦虑情绪；还有一些人则通过上网搜索技巧、阅读相关书籍，或者参加像“Toastmasters”这样的培训课程来提升自己的演讲能力。当然，还有其他解决办法（比如，新员工可以接受在职培训）。大概就是这么回事吧。

Once we know a group’s existing behaviours, it’s incredibly easy to get in touch with them. Nervous wedding speakers might be Googling for examples of great wedding speeches. We could advertise on those search terms and offer a bit of re-assurance and support in exchange for a quick chat. If someone is listening to a podcast about speaking skills, we could sponsor it or propose to the host that they run a “call in to talk about your speaking fears and solutions” live event.

一旦我们了解了某个群体的行为习惯，与他们取得联系就变得非常容易。那些在婚礼上紧张不安的演讲者，可能会在谷歌上搜索一些优秀的婚礼演讲范例。我们可以针对这些搜索词来投放广告，提供一些鼓励和支持，以换取简短的交谈。如果有人正在听关于演讲技巧的播客，我们可以赞助该播客，或者建议主持人举办一场“在线交流会，共同探讨演讲时的恐惧与解决方案”的活动。

If this was my business, I would probably shift my preferred customer segment from “students” to “people scared of public speaking who are trying to get better.” I would start by going to a Toastmasters meetup since I could have a dozen conversations over the course of the evening by Keeping it Casual. That one evening would give me a great starting point for understanding the motivations, worldview, and needs of a large group of totally ideal customers who are already spending time and money to try to get better.

如果这是我的业务，我可能会把目标客户群体从“学生”改为“那些害怕公开演讲、但希望提升自己演讲能力的人”。我会先去参加 Toastmasters 的聚会。因为在聚会上，我可以轻松地与十几个人进行交流。通过这一个晚上的接触，我就能很好地了解这些人的需求、思维方式以及动机。他们本来就愿意投入时间和金钱来提升自己的演讲能力，因此他们绝对是我理想的客户群体。

On the other hand, if you were attacking the same vision and happened to be a secret bibliophile, you might choose to start by tracking down some authors who are about to go on book tour.

另一方面，如果你也认同这一理念，同时又是个秘密的藏书爱好者，那你或许会先去寻找那些即将开始图书签售活动的作家们。

You needn’t be planning and theorising all day about this stuff — use it to quickly get to a specific, best-possible customer so you can go grab a few of them and move the business forward.

你不必整天为这些事情操心或做各种理论分析——只需利用这些方法来迅速找到最合适的客户，然后争取拿下他们，从而推动业务的发展。

Since you’ll spend a lot of time talking to your customers, it’s worth choosing someone who is both accessible and fun for you to be around. This stuff is hard work and can be a real grind if you’re cynical about the people or the industry you’re trying to understand and serve.

既然你需要花费大量时间与客户交流，那么最好选择那些容易相处、相处起来令人愉快的人作为合作伙伴。这项工作相当辛苦，如果你对那些你需要去了解和服务的人或行业持消极态度的话，那么这份工作就会变得极其繁琐。

As we mentioned, you can broaden your segment back out later. Make your life easy for now by choosing someone who is specific and who also and meets the three big criteria of being reachable, profitable, and personally rewarding.

正如我们所说，你可以在之后再扩大选择范围。现在，为了省事，不妨选择那些符合三个重要标准的人选：易于联系、能带来利润，且能带来个人成就感。

理想的客户群体应该是“谁”与“哪里”的完美结合。如果你不知道该去哪里寻找客户，那就继续将客户群体进一步细分，直到找到合适的客户群体为止。

Good customer segments are a who-where pair. If you don’t know where to go to find your customers, keep slicing your segment into smaller pieces until you do.

跟错人说话了Talking to the wrong people

You can’t get the data you need if you’re talking to the wrong people. There are 3 ways to end up fall into this clearly unhelpful trap.

如果你与错误的人交谈，就无法获得自己所需的数据。有三种方式会导致人们陷入这种毫无帮助的困境之中。

  1. You have too-broad of a segment and are talking to everyone你的目标群体范围太广了，几乎在和所有人交谈。
  2. You have multiple customer segments and missed some of them您拥有多个客户群体，但却遗漏了其中一些客户。
  1. You are selling to businesses with a complicated buying process and have overlooked some of the stakeholders你正在向那些采购流程复杂的企业销售产品，同时忽视了其中一些重要的利益相关者。

We’ve already talked about #1. If you talk to everyone, many of them are inevitably going to be the wrong people.

我们已经讨论过第 1 点。如果你试图与所有人交谈，那么其中很多人肯定不是合适的人选。

You can miss segments and overlook buyers in lots of ways. The first important step is to know they exist. Sometimes it’s obvious. If you are a multi-sided marketplace, you obviously have multiple customer segments.

你可能会以各种方式错过某些客户群体或忽视某些潜在买家。首要的关键步骤就是先意识到他们的存在。有时候，这些客户群体的存在是很明显的。如果你运营的是一个多端市场平台，那么显然就会有不同的客户群体。

Other times, it’s sneakier. If you’re building an app for kids, need to “sell” to both them and their parents. If you’re building something for public schools, you could potentially be affected by the concerns of the teachers, the students, the administration, the parent-teacher association, and the tax payers.

有时候，情况则更为复杂。如果你在为儿童开发应用程序，就需要同时“说服”儿童和他们的父母。如果你为公立学校开发产品，那么就需要考虑教师、学生、学校管理层、家长教师协会以及纳税人的各种顾虑。

You’ll also need to worry about multiple groups if you rely on an important partner, whether for manufacturing, distribution, or promotion. If your business relies on a certain partner, you’d better understand their goals and constraints just as well as you understand your customers’.

如果你依赖某个重要的合作伙伴来开展制造、分销或推广等工作，那么你还需要考虑与多个团队打交道的问题。如果你的业务离不开某个合作伙伴，那你最好能像了解自己的客户一样，充分理解该合作伙伴的目标和制约因素。

Don't just talk to the most senior or important people you can find. You want to talk to people who are representative of your customers, not ones who sound impressive on your status report. When I was building interactive advertising products, I spent lots of time talking to guys in suits and very little (well, none) talking to the teenagers we were assuming would enjoy using our products. Telling my board that I had successfully talked to ten children that week just didn’t seem like the most important thing I could be doing. In retrospect, it clearly was.

不要只与那些职位最高或最重要的人交谈。你应该与那些能够代表你目标客户群体的人交流，而不是那些在汇报中听起来很了不起的人。在开发互动广告产品时，我花了大量时间与那些穿着西装的人交谈，而几乎没怎么和那些我们认为会喜欢使用我们产品的青少年交流。当时，我觉得向董事会报告自己那周成功与十个孩子进行了交流并不是什么重要的事。但事后看来，那其实是非常重要的事。

8正在运行该进程CHAPTER EIGHT · Running the process

Even if you do everything else right, you can get bad results if you don’t have the right process wrapped around your conversations. If you just show up to meetings and hope for the best, you aren't making a good use of anyone's time. You need to do a little bit of work before and after to extract full value.

即使其他所有方面都做对了，但如果在沟通过程中没有遵循正确的流程，也很难取得好的结果。如果你只是随便参加会议，寄希望于一切顺其自然，那你就是在浪费所有人的时间。为了充分发挥各项工作的价值，你需要在会议前后做一些准备工作。

A common anti-pattern is for the business guy to go to all the meetings and subsequently tell the rest of the team what they should do. Bad idea. Telling the rest of the team “What I learned” is functionally equivalent to telling them “What you’ll do.” Therefore, owning the customer conversations creates a de-facto dictator with “The customer said so” as the ultimate trump card.

一种常见的错误做法是：让业务人员参加所有的会议，然后再告诉团队其他成员该做什么。这种做法很糟糕。把“自己从会议中了解到的信息”告诉团队其他成员，其实就相当于直接告诉他们“你们该做什么”。因此，如果让业务人员来主导与客户的沟通，那么他就会变成一个所谓的“独裁者”，而“客户这么说的”则成为他最有力的借口。

But as we’ve seen, it’s easy to misinterpret what the customer said.

但正如我们所看到的，很容易误解顾客的话。

When all the customer learning is stuck in someone’s head instead of being disseminated to the rest of the team, you’ve got a learning bottleneck. Avoid creating (or being) the bottleneck. To do that, the customer and learning has to be shared with the entire founding team, promptly and faithfully. That relies on good notes plus a bit of pre- and post-meeting work.

当所有的客户相关知识都只停留在某个人的脑海中，而没有被传达给团队中的其他人时，就会出现学习上的瓶颈。必须避免出现这种情况。为此，必须及时、准确地与整个团队分享与客户相关的信息。这需要做好记录，同时还要在会议前后做一些准备工作。

The most extreme way to bottleneck is to go to the meetings alone and take crappy notes which you don’t review with your team. At that point, your head has become the ultimate repository of customer truth and everyone just has to do what you say.

最糟糕的“瓶颈”现象就是：独自去参加会议，然后记下一些毫无价值的笔记，却从不与团队分享这些笔记。这样一来，你的头脑就成了所有关于客户信息的“唯一来源”，其他人只能按照你的指示行事。

In my case, I bottlenecked so hard that our CTO quit while saying, “We’re never going to succeed if you keep changing what we’re doing.” In my defence, the stuff I’d learned was true (at least, I think it was). But it didn’t matter anyway since I hadn’t properly communicated it to the rest of the team.

就我而言，问题严重到我们的首席技术官都辞职了。他当时说：“如果你一直不断改变我们的做事方式，那我们永远不可能成功。”不过，我得为自己辩护：我所了解到的那些道理都是正确的（至少我觉得是这样）。但不管怎样，这些道理都没能被正确地传达给团队中的其他人。

Symptoms of a learning bottleneck:

学习瓶颈的表现：

  • “You just worry about the product. I’ll learn what we need to know.”“你只需专注于产品本身吧。该了解的信息我会去弄清楚的。”
  • “Because the customers told me so!”“因为顾客们是这么告诉我的！”
  • “I don’t have time to talk to people — I need to be coding!”“我没时间跟别人聊天——我得继续写代码！”

Avoiding bottlenecks has three parts: prepping, reviewing, and taking good notes.

避免出现瓶颈需要做好三件事：提前准备、认真审查以及做好记录。

准备工作进行中。Prepping

Your most important preparation work is to ensure you know your current list of 3 big questions. Figure them out with your team and make a point to face the scary questions.

你最重要的准备工作，就是确保自己清楚目前需要解决的 3 个关键问题。与团队一起认真思考这些问题，勇于面对那些令人畏惧的难题。

If you’ve already learned the facts of your customer and industry, then you should also know what commitment and next steps you are going to push for at the end of the meeting.

如果你已经了解了关于客户和行业的基本情况，那么在会议结束时，你也应该清楚自己需要做出哪些承诺，以及接下来应该采取哪些行动。

It’s easier to guide the conversation and stay on track if you have an existing set of beliefs that you’re updating. Spend up to an hour writing down your best guesses about what the person you’re about to talk to cares about and wants. You’ll probably be wrong, but it’s easier to keep the discussion on track and hit important points if you’ve created a skeleton. If you have an appropriately focused segment, then you’ll only rarely need to do this.

如果你有一套自己认为正确的信念体系，并不断对其进行更新，那么引导对话、确保谈话不偏离主题就会容易得多。你可以花一个小时的时间，写下你对对方感兴趣和想要的东西的猜测。当然，你的猜测可能会不准确，但如果你事先做好了规划，那么就能更轻松地让对话保持正轨，从而触及到关键点。如果你能明确地确定谈话的重点，那就几乎不需要再这么做啦。

While prepping, if you come across a question which could be answered with desk research, take a moment to do it. You want to move past the obvious stuff and spend your conversations finding answers the internet can’t give you.

在准备过程中，如果你遇到那些可以通过查阅资料来解答的问题，请花点时间去解决它们。你应该跳过那些显而易见的答案，把精力集中在那些互联网无法提供的答案上。

Similarly, if you’re about to meet with a business, do your basic due diligence on LinkedIn and the company website. It takes 5 minutes and will save you from borking the conversation and looking like an idiot.

同样地，如果你即将与某家公司会面，最好先在 LinkedIn 和该公司的官方网站上做一些基本的调查。这只需要 5 分钟的时间，却能避免你犯下愚蠢的错误，让自己出丑。

Sit down with your whole founding team when you prep. You want both business and product to be represented. If you leave part of the company out of the prep, then you end up missing their concerns in the customer conversations.

在准备过程中，务必让所有创始团队成员都参与进来。无论是业务方面还是产品方面，都应有代表出席。如果让公司的一部分成员被排除在准备过程之外，那么在与客户的沟通中，就难免会忽略他们的意见和顾虑。

Some founders react with hostility to the whole idea. They’ll say something along the lines of, “We need to be building the gosh derned product, not fuffing around talking to people.” On the surface, it appears to be a refusal to face the reality that there might be something wrong with the product’s foundations. But having seen it in several teams now, I think it’s actually the desire for speed, where speed is measured by building features instead of by de-risking and validating the business.

有些创始人对这种想法持敌对态度。他们可能会说：“我们应该专注于开发产品，而不是浪费时间与别人交谈。”表面上看，这似乎是拒绝面对产品本身可能存在问题的现实。但经过在多个团队的观察，我发现，这其实是一种对速度的追求——所谓“速度”，指的是尽快开发出各种功能，而不是先消除风险、验证业务的可行性。

Beyond asking a grumpy cofounder to “humour me”, there are 2 questions to help tease out hidden product risks. The first is asking, “If this company fails, what is most likely to have killed it?” If we had asked that about my very first product (a user-generated cartoon community), we would have answered that either nobody wants to create cartoons or that their cartoons will be so bad that nobody wants to watch them. This gives us a good starting point to begin exploring with a combination of technical and conversational tools.

除了要求脾气暴躁的联合创始人“迁就我”之外，还有两个问题有助于揭示产品中潜在的风险。第一个问题是：“如果这家公司失败了，最有可能是什么原因导致的？”如果当初我们对自己的第一个产品（一个用户创作的漫画社区）提出这个问题，我们可能会认为：要么没人愿意创作漫画，要么人们创作的漫画质量太差，没人愿意看。这两个问题的答案为我们提供了良好的起点，让我们能够结合技术和沟通手段来进一步分析问题。

Prep questions to unearth hidden risks:

通过预先提出问题来揭示潜在风险：

  • If this company were to fail, why would it have happened?如果这家公司会倒闭，那究竟是为什么呢？
  • What would have to be true for this to be a huge success?要使这件事取得巨大成功，需要满足哪些条件呢？

The second question is a flipped version of the first, from Lafley/Martin’s strategy book. “What would have to be true for this to be a huge success?” In this case, I might have answered that we need lots of hilarious cartoons plus scaling revenue from brand advertisers.

第二个问题其实是第一个问题的另一种表述方式，源自拉夫利和马丁所著的策略类书籍。 “要使这件事取得巨大成功，需要满足哪些条件呢？”在这种情况下，我可能会回答：我们需要大量有趣的漫画内容，同时还要确保品牌广告带来的收入能够持续增长。

These aren’t long, involved strategy discussions. Your gut reaction is enough. You don’t need to be too rigorous. All you’re really trying to figure out is:

这些并不需要复杂的战略规划或深思熟虑。凭你的直觉行事就足够了。没必要过于繁琐。你真正需要弄清楚的只是：

  • “What do we want to learn from these guys?”“我们想从这些人身上学到什么？”

In fact, if you’re tight for time, asking this question is the only prep you really need.

事实上，如果你时间紧迫，那么提出这个问题就是你唯一需要做的准备了。

如果你不知道自己想要学习什么，那就没必要进行这样的对话了。

If you don’t know what you’re trying to learn, you shouldn’t bother having the conversation.

8.1正在审核中Reviewing

After a conversation, just review your notes with your team and update your beliefs and 3 big questions as appropriate.

谈话结束后，与团队一起回顾一下笔记，然后根据需要更新自己的观点以及那三个关键问题。

The goal is to ensure the learning is now on paper and in everyone’s head instead of just in yours. Talk through the key quotes and main takeaways of the conversation, as well as any problems you ran into.

目标是确保所学的内容不仅存储在你的脑海中，也记录在纸上，让每个人都能记住。请详细讨论一下谈话中的关键内容、主要结论，以及你在过程中遇到的任何问题。

I also like to talk about the meta-level of the conversation itself: which questions worked and which didn’t? How can we do better next time? Were there any important signals or questions we missed? This stuff is more craft than science: you have to actively practice it to get better. It’s a valuable skill for your team to have, so it’s worth spending a bit of time improving.

我还喜欢探讨对话本身的“元层面”问题：哪些问题起到了作用，哪些没有？下次我们该如何改进？有没有什么重要的信号或问题被我们忽略了？这些其实更像是需要通过实践来掌握的技巧，而非纯粹的科学原理。这对团队来说是一项非常有价值的技能，因此值得花些时间来提升它。

Just like prepping, reviewing is so simple that it sounds like a non-step. It’s tempting to skip it. Don’t! The review is important. Disseminate learnings to your team as quickly and as directly as possible, using notes and exact quotes wherever you can. It keeps you in sync, leads to better decisions, prevents arguments, and allows your whole team to benefit from the learning you’ve worked so hard to acquire.

就像准备工作一样，复习其实也非常简单，简直就像是个可有可无的步骤。人们很容易想要跳过这一步。但千万不要这么做！复习非常重要。请尽可能快、直接地将所学到的知识传达给团队成员，必要时可以使用笔记或原话来辅助说明。这样做有助于保持团队成员之间的同步，有助于做出更好的决策，避免不必要的争执，同时也能让整个团队都能从你辛苦获得的知识中受益。

谁应该出席呢Who should show up

Everyone on the team who is making big decisions (including tech decisions) needs to go to at least some of the meetings. The tech guys don’t need to go to most of the meetings, but you’ll all learn a ton from hearing customer reactions first-hand occasionally. You’ll also be able to help each other catch and fix your conversation mistakes and biases.

团队中所有需要做出重要决策的人（包括那些负责技术方面的决策的人）都应该至少参加部分会议。虽然技术人员不必参加所有的会议，但偶尔直接听取客户的反馈，对大家来说都是很有帮助的。此外，大家还可以互相帮助，纠正彼此在交流中出现的错误和偏见。

Meetings go best when you've got two people at them. One person can focus on taking notes and the other can focus on talking. As the second person, sometimes you'll notice the lead asking bad questions or missing a signal they should be digging into. Just jump in and fix them.

两个人一起开会效果最好。一个人可以专心做记录，另一个人则可以专注于发言。作为参与者，你有时会注意到，主导会议的人可能会提出一些不恰当的问题，或者忽略了一些需要深入探讨的要点。这时，你就应该及时介入并加以纠正。

Don’t send more than two people unless it’s group-on-group or you’ve got a particularly good reason to do so. Three folks interviewing someone can be a little overwhelming.

除非是团队与团队之间的面试，或者有特别充分的理由，否则不要派超过两个人去面试。三个人一起面试某个人的话，可能会让对方感到压力过大。

Going in solo is fine once you get good at taking concise notes. The main problem of running solo is that it's harder to catch yourself and recover once you start going off-track asking bad questions or missing the point.

一旦你掌握了如何做简洁有效的笔记，独自工作也没什么问题。不过，独自工作的最大缺点在于：一旦你开始问错问题或偏离了正确的方向，就很难及时发现并纠正错误。

If you’re shy and have no cofounder to take the lead, call in a favour with a friend to come to your first couple conversations with you. You can just play the role of the note-taker until you’re comfortable. If there’s no workaround and you have to bite the bullet, remember that confidence is domain-specific. Instead of suffering through, focus on getting better and understanding the process. It will get less scary as you improve.

如果你性格内向，又没有合作者可以带头行事，那就请朋友陪你一起参加最初的几次谈话吧。你可以先扮演记录员的角色，直到自己能够自如应对为止。如果实在没有别的办法，只能硬着头皮去面对，那么请记住：自信是因领域而异的。不要一味忍受痛苦，而是要专注于提升自己、了解整个过程。随着能力的提升，你会觉得没那么可怕了。

You can't outsource or hire someone to do customer learning. There are exceptional team dynamics where it works, but generally speaking, the founders need to be in the meetings themselves. When a hired gun brings you bad news (“This problem isn't real and nobody cares about what we're doing”), properly assimilating it is difficult.

你无法将了解客户的需求这项工作外包或委托给别人来完成。虽然在某些情况下，由专门的团队来处理这项工作会更有效率，但总体而言，创始人必须亲自参与这些会议。当那些受雇来处理这些问题的人带来坏消息时（比如“这个问题并不存在，也没人关心我们在做什么”），就很难妥善地应对这些信息了。

More insidiously, if the signal is lukewarm, it’s tempting for a hire to gloss over it. Hiring out your learning is a guaranteed way to get bad signals. Until you've got a working business model and a repeatable sales or marketing process, the founders need to be in the meetings themselves.

更隐蔽的问题是，如果反馈信号并不积极，那么招聘人员很可能会选择忽略这些信号。将学习成果外包出去，只会让反馈信号变得更差。在建立起可行的商业模式以及可重复的销售或营销流程之前，创始人必须亲自参与各项会议。

On the bright side, even though you have to be in the room to process the learning, you don't necessarily have to be the one setting up or leading the meetings. You can hire people to help as long as you’re there with them, listening.

从积极的角度来看，虽然你必须在场才能完成学习过程，但你并不一定非得亲自组织和主持会议。你可以雇佣他人来帮忙，只要你自己能在场并认真倾听即可。

8.2该如何把它记录下来呢How to write it down

Taking good notes is the best way to keep your team (and investors and advisors) in the loop. Plus, notes make it harder to lie to yourself. And when, months later, you decide to adjust the business's direction, you'll be able to return to your notes instead of having to go do a whole new set of interviews.

做好笔记是让团队成员、投资者和顾问随时了解情况的最佳方式。此外，笔记还能帮助你避免自欺欺人。几个月后，当你决定调整业务方向时，只需参考之前的笔记即可，无需再重新进行一系列的访谈。

When possible, write down exact quotes. Wrap them in quotation marks so you know it's verbatim. You can later use them in your marketing language, fundraising decks, and to resolve arguments with skeptical teammates. Other times the exact quote isn't relevant and you just write down the big idea.

如果可能的话，请把原文照抄下来。用引号把引文括起来，这样就能确保内容的准确性。之后，你可以把这些引文用在营销文案、筹款材料中，或者用来反驳那些持怀疑态度的同事。当然，有时候原文并不重要，只需记下核心观点即可。

In either case, add symbols to your notes as context and shorthand. I use 12 main symbols and make up more in the field as I need them. You probably won't exactly copy mine, so don't worry about memorising them. You'll build your own go-to list as you have more conversations.

无论哪种情况，都可以在笔记中添加各种符号，作为辅助说明或缩写。我使用了 12 个主要的符号，另外还会根据需要随时创造新的符号。你不必完全照搬我的做法，所以不必刻意去记住这些符号。随着对话的增多，你会逐渐形成属于自己的符号体系。

:) :( :| ☇ ⨅ ☐ ⤴ ^ ☑＄♀☆

Emotions

情绪/情感

:)Excited

:)太兴奋了

:( Angry

:( 愤怒

:|Embarrassed

:|尴尬极了

Someone saying "that's a problem" should be interpreted totally differently depending on whether they are neutral or outraged. Any strong emotion is worth writing down. For example, depending on your industry, you might also choose to make symbols for lust or laughter. Capture the big emotions and remember to dig into them when they come up.

当有人说“那是个问题”时，我们需要根据其态度是中性还是愤怒来理解其含义。任何强烈的情绪都值得被记录下来。例如，根据你所处的行业，你也可以选择用特定的符号来表示欲望或笑声。请把那些强烈的情绪记录下来，以后再仔细思考它们。

Their life

他们的生活

☇ Pain or problem (symbol is a lightning bolt)

☇ 疼痛或问题所在（符号为闪电形状）

⨅ Goal or job-to-be-done (symbol is a soccer/football goal)

⨅ 目标或需要完成的任务（用足球门作为符号表示）

☐ Obstacle

☐ 障碍物/障碍

⤴Workaround

⤴ 暂时解决方案/临时应对方法

^Background or context (symbol is a distant mountain)

^背景或语境（符号代表远处的山脉）

These five “life” symbols are your bread and butter. Combine them with emotion symbols where appropriate. Pains and obstacles carry a lot more weight when someone is embarrassed or angry about them.

这五个“生命”符号代表着你的生计来源。在适当的情况下，可以将它们与表达情感的符号结合起来使用。当人们因为某些痛苦或障碍而感到尴尬或愤怒时，这些痛苦和障碍所带来的影响就会更大。

Obstacles are preventing a customer from solving their problems even though they want to. They're important because you'll probably also have to deal with them. For example, a lot of corporate folks would love to use cloud services and hate their current options, but can't use anything else because their company's IT policy is an obstacle. Their workaround might be to use their personal phone as a secondary computer or by doing certain work at home. Also worth noting.

各种障碍使得客户即便有解决问题的意愿，也无法实现。这些障碍很重要，因为您很可能也需要去应对它们。例如，很多企业员工很想使用云服务，但又不满意现有的选择。然而，由于公司的 IT 政策限制，他们无法使用其他方案。这时，他们可能会用个人手机作为辅助设备来处理工作，或者选择在家工作。这些情况都值得注意。

Specifics

具体细节/具体情况

☑ Feature request or purchasing criteria

☑ 功能需求或购买标准

＄Money or budgets or purchasing process

金钱、预算或采购流程

♀ Mentioned a specific person or company

♀ 提到了某个特定的人或公司

☆ Follow-up task

☆ 后续任务

As we discussed, feature requests usually get ignored, but they're a good signal to capture and explore. Must-have purchasing criteria are obviously more important. Money signals are also key.

正如我们所讨论的，关于功能的各种需求通常会被忽视，但这些需求其实值得被记录下来并进一步探讨。当然，那些不可或缺的购买标准则更为重要。此外，价格因素也是不可忽视的关键因素。

Write down specific names and companies. If it’s someone they know, ask for an intro at the end of the conversation. If it’s a competitor or alternate solution, write it down to research it later.

请把具体的姓名和公司名称记下来。如果对方是他们认识的人，可以在谈话结束时请对方帮忙引荐。如果是竞争对手或可行的替代方案，也请把相关信息记下来，以便日后进一步了解。

Put a big star on items to follow-up on after the meeting, especially next steps you promised as a condition of their advancement/commitment.

在会议后需要跟进的事项上标上星号，尤其是那些作为项目推进/承诺的必要条件而需要你采取的行动。

该把内容写在哪儿呢？Where to write it down

You want to take your customer notes so that they are:

你需要妥善记录客户的各项信息，以便：

  • Able to be sorted, mixed, and re-arranged可以对其进行排序、混合和重新排列。
  • Able to be combined with the notes of the rest of your team可以与团队中其他成员的音符相互配合/可以与团队整体节奏相协调
  • Permanent and retrievable永久性且可恢复的
  • Not mixed in with other random noise like todo lists and ideas不会与其他杂乱无章的内容混在一起，比如待办事项列表或各种想法之类的。

In practice, I’ve found that taking notes in my primary notebook is practically useless. Over the course of several months’ sprawl, you set an insurmountable search and retrieval task for yourself. If you won’t look at your notes, they aren’t much good.

实际上，我发现把笔记记在主笔记本上几乎毫无用处。几个月下来，笔记会变得杂乱无章，查找和检索这些笔记会变得极其困难。如果你都不愿意去查看自己的笔记，那它们就毫无价值了。

If you do end up taking notes in non-ideal places, you just need to spend a little extra post-meeting time moving them over into your permanent format. Otherwise you’ll be kicking yourself when you remember that amazing quote someone gave you which you can’t quite remember well enough to convince those skeptical investors.

如果你不得不在不太理想的环境中做笔记，那么会议结束后只需花一点时间把笔记整理成便于长期保存的格式即可。否则，当你想起某人说的那句很棒的話，却又因为记不清而无法向那些持怀疑态度的投资者解释时，你一定会后悔的。

What is a better note-taking medium?

什么是更好的笔记记录方式呢？

Google Docs spreadsheets and Evernote are both great for team sharing, search, and retrieval. Spreadsheets are wonderfully sortable if you write your key signals in their own column. The downside is that it comes across as rude to take notes directly on a computer during a meeting, so you add a bit of mandatory post-meeting work to transfer your notes. I always found this a bit annoying, but it’s way better than losing your learning in an off-topic notebook.

Google Docs 中的电子表格功能以及 Evernote 都非常适合团队协作、信息检索和查找。如果把关键信息分别记录在不同的列中，那么对数据进行排序会变得非常方便。不过，缺点是：在会议期间直接在电脑上做笔记显得不太礼貌。因此，不得不在会议结束后再花些时间把笔记转录下来。虽然这有点麻烦，但总比把学到的知识记在无关的笔记本里要好得多。

A dedicated notebook is fine, if you can remember to carry it with you and use it. For a while, I would use my regular notebook for my customer notes and then just tear out every page which wasn’t from an interview so it was easier to find them later. Hardly the most elegant.

如果能够记得随身携带并使用的话，用普通的笔记本也完全没问题。有一段时间，我会把客户信息记在普通笔记本上，之后把那些与面试无关的页面撕掉，这样以后查找起来会更方便。不过，这种方式实在不算什么高明的办法。

My favourite medium is index cards. Post-it notes also work. I carry around blank cards and take notes on them, with one quote or learning per card (along with a signal icon, of course). After the meeting I jot down the date and name of the person on each card. It takes a couple minutes, but it’s faster than moving over all your notes. When we review, we lay the cards out on the table and sort through them. Cards are great because of the random access and sorting. If you learn that the problem you’re solving doesn’t really matter, you can just pull out all the cards marked with a lightning bolt and find yourself a new problem that’s already been confirmed.

我最喜欢的记录工具是索引卡。便利贴也可以用来做记录。我会随身携带空白卡片，在上面记录各种信息：每张卡片上写一句格言或一条学习心得（当然，还会加上相应的图标）。会议结束后，我会在每张卡片上写下日期和涉及的人的名字。虽然这样做需要几分钟时间，但比把所有笔记都抄录下来要快得多。在回顾笔记时，我们可以把卡片摊在桌子上进行整理。索引卡的优点在于可以方便地查找和分类信息。如果你发现正在解决的问题其实并不重要，只需把那些标有闪电符号的卡片抽出来，就能找到另一个已经被确认为值得解决的问题。

When I first started, I would make audio recordings, but that suffered from the same problem as notebooks. I'd end up with a ton of content and no real way to sort it or find the bit I wanted, so I ended up ignoring the recordings.

刚开始的时候，我会把音频内容录下来。但这种方法也面临着与笔记本记录同样的问题：虽然我记录了大量的内容，却无法有效地整理这些内容，也无法找到自己想要的信息。因此，我最终放弃了这种录音方式。

If you do want to use audio, you’ll find people are surprisingly willing to be recorded. Put your phone on the table as you sit down and say, “Hey, is it cool if I record this, because I’m always paranoid I’ll miss something important in my notes. It won’t get shared with anyone or posted anywhere.” Almost everyone says yes, but it does blow your cover if you’re trying to Keep it Casual.

如果你确实想使用音频记录，你会发现人们出人意料地愿意被录音。坐下时把手机放在桌上，然后说：“嘿，我可以把这次谈话录下来吗？我总担心会错过一些重要的内容。这些录音不会被分享给任何人，也不会被公开。”几乎每个人都会同意。不过，如果你想保持低调的话，这么做反而会暴露你的意图。

If you embrace Keeping it Casual, you'll find customer conversations appearing out of nowhere and catching you by surprise. Just go with it. I've got notes written on paper plates from a pizza party, on beer mats from the pub, and on newspapers from the cafe. You'll want to transfer them over to your main storage system once you get back to the office, but it's better to capture what's said on something weird than to try to remember all the important bits.

如果你选择采取“随性记录”的方式，你会发现，客户的对话会不知不觉地被记录下来，让你措手不及。那就顺其自然吧。我在披萨派对上把笔记写在纸盘上，在酒吧里则把笔记写在啤酒杯垫上，而在咖啡馆里则把笔记写在报纸上。回到办公室后，你当然可以把这些笔记转移到正式的存储系统中。不过，与其试图记住所有重要的内容，不如把信息记录在随手可得的物品上。

If it’s totally inappropriate to take notes during the chat, just have the conversation and then immediately retreat to a corner to write down what was said. This is how I usually handle customer conversations at the pub or conferences.

如果在聊天过程中做笔记是完全不合适的，那就先进行对话，之后立刻找个角落把所说的内容记下来。这正是我在酒吧或会议上与客户交流时通常采用的方法。

Regardless of the medium, the most important thing is that the notes are lightweight enough that you'll actually review them yourself and with your team.

无论使用何种媒介，最重要的是，这些笔记的篇幅要足够简短，这样你和你的团队才能真正花时间去仔细阅读它们。

如果不看笔记，那笔记就毫无用处。

Notes are useless if you don't look at them.

这个过程/该步骤The process

Talking to customers is a tool, not an obligation. If it’s not going to help or you don’t want to do it for whatever reason, just skip it. I’m sure you’ve been on the receiving end of a half-assed survey sent out by some new startup to tick the box marked “learn from customers” on their startup todo list. There are better ways to waste your time.

与客户交谈只是一种手段，而非一种义务。如果这样做毫无意义，或者你出于某种原因不想这么做，那就干脆跳过吧。我敢肯定，你肯定也收到过一些初创公司发来的敷衍了事的调查问卷，他们这么做只是为了在待办事项清单上勾选“从客户那里获取反馈”这一项而已。其实，还有更值得浪费时间的方式呢。

Without figuring out what actually matters to your company and how to deal with it effectively, you’re just going through the motions.

如果不弄清楚对公司来说什么才是真正重要的，以及如何有效地应对这些问题，那只不过是在做无用功罢了。

When it comes to conversations, it looks like this:

在交谈时，情况大概是这样的：

Signs you’re just going through the motions:

表明你只是在应付了事的迹象：

  • You’re talking more than they are你说的话比他们多。
  • They are complimenting you or your idea他们是在称赞你或你的想法。
  • You told them about your idea and don’t know what’s happening next你把你的想法告诉了他们，但现在不知道接下来会发生什么。
  • You don’t have notes你没有做笔记。
  • You haven’t looked through your notes with your team你还没有和团队一起仔细查看过笔记。
  • You got an unexpected answer and it didn’t change your idea你得到了一个出乎意料的回答，但这并没有改变你的想法。
  • You weren’t scared of any of the questions you asked你对自己提出的每一个问题都不感到害怕。
  • You aren’t sure which big question you’re trying to answer by doing this你不确定自己这么做究竟是为了回答哪个重要问题。

The persistent presence of any of these problems suggests that you’re doing something wrong and wasting your time.

如果这些问题一直存在，那就说明你做错了什么，正在浪费自己的时间。

Here are the steps I go through to keep on track. Feel free to ignore or tweak as needed given your situation and company. It’s as light-weight as I’ve been able to get it and should reduce rather than increase the amount of time you need to spend on conversations.

以下是我为了保持工作进度而采取的步骤。当然，您可以根据自己的情况和公司需求来调整或忽略其中的一些步骤。这些步骤已经尽可能简化了，应该能帮助您节省时间，而不是增加您在沟通上所花费的时间。

The process before a batch of conversations:

在处理一批对话之前需要进行的步骤：

  • If you haven’t yet, choose a focused, findable segment如果你还没有这么做的话，那就选择一个明确、容易找到的目标领域吧。
  • With your team, decide your big 3 learning goals与你的团队一起，确定三个最重要的学习目标。
  • If relevant, decide on ideal next steps and commitments如有必要，确定接下来的理想行动步骤和需要履行的承诺。
  • If conversations are the right tool, figure out who to talk to如果交谈是解决问题的正确方式，那就先弄清楚该和谁交谈。
  • Create a series of best guesses about what the person cares about试着猜测一下，这个人到底在乎什么。
  • If a question could be answered via desk research, do that first如果某个问题可以通过案头研究来解答，那就先这么做吧。

During the conversation:

在交谈过程中：

  • Frame the conversation给对话设定一个框架/给谈话定下基调
  • Keep it casual保持轻松随意的态度就好。
  • Ask good questions which pass The Mom Test提出那些能够通过“妈妈测试”的好问题吧。
  • Deflect compliments, anchor fluff, and dig beneath signals回避赞美之词，剔除无谓的废话，深入探究背后的真实意图。
  • Take good notes做好笔记。
  • If relevant, press for commitment and next steps如有必要，要争取对方做出明确承诺并确定后续行动。

After a batch of conversations:

经过一番交谈之后：

  • With your team, review your notes and key customer quotes与团队一起，回顾一下笔记以及重要客户的反馈意见。
  • If relevant, transfer notes into permanent storage如有必要，将相关记录转入永久存储机制中。
  • Update your beliefs and plans更新你的想法和计划吧。
  • Decide on the next 3 big questions确定接下来的 3 个重要问题。

The goal of this process is twofold. Firstly, to make sure you're spending your time well by attacking the questions which really matter and using the whole founding team’s brains. Secondly, to spread any new learning through your team as quickly and completely as possible.

这一过程有两个目的。首先，确保你把时间花在真正重要的事情上，充分利用整个团队的智慧来解决问题。其次，尽可能快速、完整地将新学到的知识传递给团队中的每个人。

There you go. Now you know everything I do about how to learn from conversations. Combine this process with The Mom Test, Keeping it Casual, and Advancement for maximum learning in minimum time. But even if it goes wrong, don’t worry so much. Eternity will forgive.

好了，现在你已经了解了我所掌握的所有关于如何从对话中学习的技巧了。把这种方法与“妈妈测试”、“保持轻松的氛围”以及“循序渐进的学习方式”结合起来，就能在最短的时间内获得最大的学习效果。不过，就算出了差错也没关系。时间会原谅一切的。

这东西速度真快。This stuff is fast

The time scales of the process are important. The point is to make your business move faster, not slower.

该过程的节奏非常重要。关键在于让业务发展得更快，而不是更慢。

Don’t spend a week prepping for meetings; spend an hour and then go talk to people. Anything more is stalling.

不要花一周的时间来为会议做准备；只需花一小时时间，然后去与人交谈即可。再花更多的时间纯粹是在浪费时间而已。

Don’t spend months doing full-time customer conversations before beginning to move on a product. Spend a week, maybe two. Get your bearings and then give them something to commit to.

在开始着手开发某个产品之前，不必花费数月时间来与客户进行大量的沟通。花上一两周的时间吧，先了解清楚情况，然后再给出客户可以接受的方案。

You’ll keep talking to customers for the life of your company, of course. I’m not advising you to become a recluse after the first two weeks. Your customers are a crucial source of ongoing insight and will help catch any badgers in the bush, so to speak. With the tools in this book (especially Keeping it Casual) you should be able to keep benefitting from customer learning without spending any real time on it. You can do it alongside growing your business rather than in place of it.

当然，在公司存在的整个期间，你都需要持续与客户保持沟通。我并不是建议你在前两周后就不再与客户联系。客户是获取持续有用信息的重要来源，他们还能帮助你发现那些潜在的问题。利用本书中的各种方法（尤其是“保持轻松的态度”这一建议），你无需花费太多时间，就能持续从与客户的互动中获益。你可以把这项工作与业务发展同步进行，而不是将其视为业务发展的阻碍。

This stuff moves really quickly when you’re doing it right. This book isn’t meant you an excuse to squander precious months on theorising. It’s meant to help you extract maximum value in minimum time from conversations so you can get back to what really matters: building your business.

如果操作得当，这个方法的效果会非常显著。这本书并非为了让你有借口把宝贵的时间浪费在各种理论研究中。它的目的是帮助你用最短的时间从对话中获取最大价值，从而让你能够专注于真正重要的事情：发展自己的事业。

经验之谈：赶紧去创办你自己的公司吧。

Go build your dang company already.

9结论与要点总结Conclusion and cheatsheet

I still ask dumb questions all the time. You will too. Don’t beat yourself up over it. In fact, just yesterday I screwed up a particularly important meeting by slipping into pitch mode (this was yesterday at the time of writing… hopefully not again at the time of reading).

我仍然会时不时地问一些愚蠢的问题。你也会如此的。不必为此自责。事实上，就在昨天，因为我不小心进入了“推销模式”，结果搞砸了一次非常重要的会议（写这篇文章的时候是昨天……希望读到这篇文章的时候不会再发生这种事了）。

It happens. It’s okay. Review with your team, don’t beat yourself up (or your cofounders) over mistakes. Work on getting better as a team.

这种事情难免会发生。没关系的。与团队一起回顾一下发生的问题，不要因为犯错而自责或责备共同创业的伙伴们。作为团队，要努力变得更好。

I make tons of mistakes. At least now I notice and have a chance to fix them. Most bad conversations can be fixed. You’re trying to do something difficult. You’re never going to be perfect, but it always helps to be better.

我经常犯错。不过现在，我能够意识到自己的错误，并有机会去纠正它们。大多数糟糕的对话都是可以挽回的。你正在尝试做一件困难的事情。你永远不可能做到完美，但不断努力变得更好总归是有帮助的。

People love startups. Startups do cool stuff and make their lives better. Everyone supports the entrepreneur. When entrepreneurs screw up, people want to forgive them. They want the entrepreneur and the startup to succeed.

人们喜欢初创企业。初创企业做着很棒的事情，让人们的生活变得更美好。大家都支持创业者。当创业者犯错时，人们愿意原谅他们。大家都希望创业者和初创企业能够取得成功。

By asking good questions, we can fix many flawed ideas before they get us in trouble. But still, sometimes it doesn’t work out. It’s okay. Go to the people who believed in and supported you and thank them. If relevant, apologise. They’ll say something like:

通过提出恰当的问题，我们可以在各种有问题的想法造成麻烦之前将其纠正过来。不过，有时候这样做也无效。没关系。去找那些信任和支持你的人，向他们表示感谢。如果有必要的话，也要道歉。他们可能会这么说：

“Hey, don’t even worry about it — I know how it goes.”

“嘿，别担心了——我明白是怎么回事的。”

Eternity will forgive our earthly blunders. Everyone loves an entrepreneur. After all, you’re putting it on the line to try and make their lives better.

永恒会原谅我们在尘世中的种种过错。大家都喜欢企业家。毕竟，企业家愿意冒风险去努力改善人们的生活。

应该没问题的。

It’s going to be okay.

破解它吧Hack it

In ancient times it was prophesied that whoever undid the convoluted Gordian Knot would rule the land. Coming across the knot, Alexander drew his sword and with a single stroke, cut the knot in half. The knot was undone and he became “the Great”.

在古代，有预言称：谁能解开那个复杂的戈尔迪安之结，谁就能统治这片土地。当亚历山大遇到这个结时，他拔出剑，一剑将之斩断。如此，那个复杂的结终于被解开了，而亚历山大也因此成为了“伟大的君主”。

Struggling to untie the knot was the “proper” process. Cutting through it was a hack (ba-dum tish!). Alexander jumped straight to the end result without any of the work.

试图解开绳结才是“正确的”做法。直接剪断绳结则是一种偷懒的做法罢了。亚历山大直接跳过了所有步骤，直接得到了最终结果。

When we find a new and exciting process, it’s easy to spend hours obsessively geeking out about exactly the right way to untie this big, gnarly knot.

当我们发现某个新颖而有趣的过程时，很容易会花上好几个小时来仔细思考：究竟该如何才能解开这个复杂的难题。

At a workshop about customer segmentation, a personal trainer said he was spending most of his time on the non-billable commutes between his clients. At some point it was suggested that the police might be a better customer, since he could go to the station and spend all day working with different officers. No commute, more billable hours, and a less price-sensitive customer. Sounds good in theory.

在一个关于客户细分的研讨会上，一位私人教练表示，他大部分时间都浪费在往返于客户住所之间的路上，而这些时间是无法计费的工作时间。有人建议，警察或许会是更好的客户群体——因为他可以前往警察局，一整天都与不同的警察一起工作。这样既不需要浪费时间在通勤上，又能增加可计费的工作时间。而且，警察对价格也不那么敏感。从理论上来说，这听起来不错。

We all started geeking out about how he was going to validate this. Who could he talk to? Did anyone have family in the police? What should his interview questions be? What was the market size? Etc etc.

我们都开始好奇他打算如何来验证这一点了。他该和谁交谈呢？他的家人中有在警察部门工作的人吗？他应该提出哪些问题呢？该市场的规模又有多大呢？等等。

He looked at us like we were all idiots and held up his phone. “Why wouldn’t I just call them?” It’s not like he didn’t know their number. We all stood around a bit dumbfounded while he went outside and called the police. Twenty minutes later, he came back in with a trial session scheduled.

他看着我们，好像我们都是白痴一样，然后举起手机说：“我为什么不直接给他们打电话呢？”他明明知道他们的电话号码。我们都呆呆地站在那里，而他则出去打电话报警了。二十分钟后，他回来告诉我们，警方已经安排了听证会的时间。

Having a process is valuable, but don’t get stuck in it. Sometimes you can just pick up the phone and hack through the knot.

拥有明确的流程固然很重要，但不要陷入其中无法自拔。有时候，只需拿起电话，就能解决问题。

9.1作弊指南/捷径大全Cheatsheet

Just in case you like lists.

以防你喜欢列表形式的内容。

Key skills:

关键技能：

  • Asking good questions (Chapters 1 & 3)提出恰当的问题（第 1 章和第 3 章）
  • Avoiding bad data (Chapter 2)避免使用不良数据（第 2 章）
  • Keeping it casual (Chapter 4)保持轻松自在的态度（第 4 章）
  • Pushing for commitment & advancement (Chapter 5)推动承诺的履行与进展（第五章）
  • Framing the meeting (Chapter 6)会议的安排与筹备（第 6 章）
  • Customer segmentation (Chapter 7)客户细分（第 7 章）
  • Prepping & reviewing (Chapter 8)准备与复习（第 8 章）
  • Taking notes (Chapter 8)做笔记（第 8 章）

The Mom Test:

“妈妈测试”：

  1. Talk about their life instead of your idea聊聊他们的生活吧，而不是你的想法。
  2. Ask about specifics in the past instead of generics or opinions about the future询问过去的具体情况，而不是那些笼统的描述或对未来的看法。
  3. Talk less and listen more少说多听。

Getting back on track (avoiding bad data):

重回正轨（避免使用错误的数据）：

  • Deflect compliments拒绝接受赞美/不接受恭维的话
  • Anchor fluff锚形绒毛/类似锚形状的绒毛状物
  • Dig beneath opinions, ideas, requests, and emotions深入探究那些观点、想法、请求和情感背后的真实含义。

Mistakes and symptoms:

错误与症状：

  1. Fishing for compliments想得到赞美/想被人夸奖

“I’m thinking of starting a business... so, do you think it will work?”

“我在考虑创业……你觉得能成功吗？”

“I had an awesome idea for an app — do you like it?”

“我有个很棒的应用程序创意——你喜欢吗？”

  1. Exposing your ego (aka The Pathos Problem)暴露自己的自负心理（也就是所谓的“情感问题”）
  • “So here’s that top-secret project I quit my job for... what do you think?”“这就是我为了而辞去工作的那个绝密项目……你觉得怎么样？”
  • “I can take it — be honest and tell me what you really think!”“我能承受的——请诚实一点，告诉我你的真实想法吧！”
  1. Being pitchy声音太尖了/音调太高了
  • “No no, I don’t think you get it...”“不不，我觉得你没明白我的意思……”
  • “Yes, but it also does this!”“没错，但它还有别的功能呢！”
  1. Being too formal过于正式了
  • “So, first off, thanks for agreeing to this interview. I just have a few questions for you and then I’ll let you get back to your day…”“首先，感谢您同意接受这次采访。我有一些问题想问您，之后您就可以继续处理自己的事务了……”
  • “On a scale of 1 to 5, how much would you say you…”“在 1 到 5 的评分范围内，您认为自己的程度是多少呢？”
  • “Let’s set up a meeting.”“我们安排一次会面吧。”
  1. Being a learning bottleneck属于学习上的瓶颈/是学习过程中的障碍
  • “You just worry about the product. I’ll learn what we need to know.”“你只需专注于产品本身吧。该了解的信息我会去弄清楚的。”
  • “Because the customers told me so!”“因为顾客们是这么告诉我的！”
  • “I don’t have time to talk to people — I need to be coding!“我没时间跟别人聊天——我得继续写代码！”
  1. Collecting compliments instead of facts and commitments只收集赞美之词，而不关注事实和承诺。
  • “We’re getting a lot of positive feedback.”“我们收到了很多积极的反馈。”
  • “Everybody I’ve talked to loves the idea.”“我与之交谈过的每个人都很喜欢这个主意。”

The process before, during and after the meeting:

会议召开之前、期间以及之后的整个流程：

  • If you haven’t yet, choose a focused, findable segment如果你还没有这么做的话，那就选择一个明确、容易找到的目标领域吧。
  • With your team, decide your big 3 learning goals与你的团队一起，确定三个最重要的学习目标。
  • If relevant, decide on ideal next steps and commitments如有必要，确定接下来的理想行动步骤和需要履行的承诺。
  • If conversations are the right tool, figure out who to talk to如果交谈是解决问题的正确方式，那就先弄清楚该和谁交谈。
  • Create a series of best guesses about what the person cares about试着猜测一下，这个人到底在乎什么。
  • If a question could be answered via desk research, do that first如果某个问题可以通过案头研究来解答，那就先这么做吧。
  • Frame the conversation给对话设定一个框架/给谈话定下基调
  • Keep it casual保持轻松随意的态度就好。
  • Ask good questions which pass The Mom Test提出那些能够通过“妈妈测试”的好问题吧。
  • Deflect compliments, anchor fluff, and dig beneath signals回避赞美之词，剔除无谓的废话，深入探究背后的真实意图。
  • Take good notes做好笔记。
  • If relevant, press for commitment and next steps如有必要，要争取对方做出明确承诺并确定后续行动。
  • With your team, review your notes and key customer quotes与团队一起，回顾一下笔记以及重要客户的反馈意见。
  • If relevant, transfer notes into permanent storage如有必要，将相关记录转入永久存储机制中。
  • Update your beliefs and plans更新你的想法和计划吧。
  • Decide on the next 3 big questions确定接下来的 3 个重要问题。

Results of a good meeting:

一次良好会议的成果：

  • Facts — concrete, specific facts about what they do and why they do it (as opposed to the bad data of compliments, fluff, and opinions)事实——关于他们所做的事情以及为何要这么做的一些具体、明确的事实（而不是那些毫无价值的恭维话、废话或主观意见）。
  • Commitment — They are showing they’re serious by giving up something they value such as meaningful amounts of time, reputational risk, or money.承诺——他们通过放弃自己看重的东西来表明自己的诚意，比如宝贵的时间、可能带来的声誉风险或金钱。
  • Advancement — They are moving to the next step of your real-world funnel and getting closer to a sale.进展——他们正在进入销售流程的下一阶段，距离完成销售越来越近了。

Signs you’re just going through the motions:

表明你只是在应付了事的迹象：

  • You’re talking more than they are你说的话比他们多。
  • They are complimenting you or your idea他们是在称赞你或你的想法。
  • You told them about your idea and don’t know what’s happening next你把你的想法告诉了他们，但现在不知道接下来会发生什么。
  • You don’t have notes你没有做笔记。
  • You haven’t looked through your notes with your team你还没有和团队一起仔细查看过笔记。
  • You got an unexpected answer and it didn’t change your idea你得到了一个出乎意料的回答，但这并没有改变你的想法。
  • You weren’t scared of any of the questions you asked你对自己提出的每一个问题都不感到害怕。
  • You aren’t sure which big question you’re trying to answer by doing this你不确定自己这么做究竟是为了回答哪个重要问题。
  • You aren’t sure why you’re having the meeting你不确定召开这次会议的真正原因是什么。

Writing it down — signal symbols:

把它们写下来——信号符号：

:)Excited

:)太兴奋了

:( Angry

:( 愤怒

:|Embarrassed

:|尴尬极了

☇ Pain or problem (symbol is a lightning bolt)

☇ 疼痛或问题所在（符号为闪电形状）

⨅ Goal or job-to-be-done (symbol is a soccer/football goal)

⨅ 目标或需要完成的任务（用足球门作为符号表示）

☐ Obstacle

☐ 障碍物/障碍

⤴Workaround

⤴ 暂时解决方案/临时应对方法

^Background or context (symbol is a distant mountain)

^背景或语境（符号代表远处的山脉）

☑ Feature request or purchasing criteria

☑ 功能需求或购买标准

＄Money or budgets or purchasing process

金钱、预算或采购流程

♀ Mentioned a specific person or company

♀ 提到了某个特定的人或公司

☆ Follow-up task

☆ 后续任务

Signs you aren’t pushing for commitment and advancement:

表明你不愿做出承诺或寻求进一步发展的迹象：

  • A pipeline of zombie leads一串串毫无价值的线索/一堆毫无用处的信息
  • Ending product meetings with a compliment以赞美的话语结束产品会议
  • Ending product meetings with no clear next steps以没有明确后续行动的方式结束产品会议
  • Meetings which "went well"“进行顺利”的会议/那些“没有出问题的”会议
  • They haven't given up anything of value他们没有放弃任何有价值的东西。

Asking for and framing the meeting:

提出会议请求并确定会议议程：

  • Vision — half-sentence version of how you’re making the world better愿景——用半句话来描述你如何让世界变得更美好
  • Framing — where you’re at and what you’re looking for框架设定——你当前所处的位置以及你所寻求的目标是什么
  • Weakness — show how you can be helped弱点——说明自己需要怎样的帮助/请指出自己的不足之处
  • Pedestal — show that they, in particular, can provide that help基座——表明他们完全有能力提供那种帮助。
  • Ask — ask for help寻求帮助——请求别人的协助。

The big prep question:

最重要的准备工作是：

  • “What do we want to learn from these guys?”“我们想从这些人身上学到什么？”

9.2谢谢！Thanks!

It’s been fun. Thanks for reading :). I hope it was helpful. For slides, videos, and other information, check out momtestbook.com.

过程很有趣。感谢阅读：）。希望这些内容对您有所帮助。如需查看幻灯片、视频及其他相关资料，请访问 momtestbook.com。

When you hit a stumbling block I can help with or learn that I gave some bad advice, hit me up on twitter @robfitz.

如果你遇到了障碍，我可以提供帮助；或者，如果是我给出了错误的建议，也请在 Twitter 上@robfitz 与我联系吧。

My blog where I write about early stage startup stuff is thestartuptoolkit.com. Subscribe to the rss or email updates and dig through the archive.

我在 thestartuptoolkit.com 上撰写关于初创企业的相关文章。您可以订阅该网站的 RSS 或电子邮件更新服务，同时也可以浏览过去的文章。

To learn to be a better founder, you’ve got to get your hands dirty. Startups are more craft than science. It’s something you learn by doing. The tools help, but you need to get out in the field. I’ve learned more from launching silly projects than I ever did from reading books or blogs. Make it happen.

要想成为更出色的创业者，就必须亲自动手去实践。创业更像是一门手艺，而非纯粹的科学。只有通过实践才能真正学会。虽然各种工具能提供帮助，但你必须亲自去行动。我从那些看似愚蠢的项目中所学到的，远远超过从书本或博客中获得的知识。那就行动起来吧。

And finally, if you’re in the business of supporting, training, mentoring, or investing in startups, I work with a few other founders building educational curriculum (slides, workshops, videos, facilitation guides, etc) at foundercentric.com. Download resources, join the mailing list, and get involved. We’ve run training from Costa Rica to Kiev with folks ranging from Oxford to the UN. Get in touch at aloha@foundercentric.com.

最后，如果您从事支持、培训、指导或投资初创企业的工作，我与几位创始人一起在 foundercentric.com 网站上开发各种教学资源，包括幻灯片、培训课程、视频、指导手册等。您可以下载这些资源，加入邮件列表，积极参与其中。我们的培训活动从哥斯达黎加一直举办到基辅，参与者来自牛津大学、联合国等机构。如需联系我们，请发送邮件至 aloha@foundercentric.com。

没有人能够独自度过创业的艰难历程。感谢保罗·格雷厄姆在我们毫无头绪的时候仍然愿意支持我们；也感谢德文、劳伦斯和瑞安愿意加入这场疯狂的冒险。感谢索尔·克莱因每周五都耐心地指导我关于销售方面的问题，还强迫我认真阅读《通往顿悟的四个步骤》这本书。当然，也要感谢史蒂夫·布兰克撰写了这本书。最后，感谢彼得·里德和安迪·C 向我展示了销售的真正运作方式。

感谢伊恩和皮特帮助我们在伦敦安顿下来。感谢我在 Braintrust 团队的同事们，他们总是愿意倾听我的想法；尤其要感谢萨利姆·维拉尼，他总是能指出我的谬误。感谢汤姆·斯通、戴夫·查普曼、约翰·斯平德勒和蒂姆·巴恩斯，他们让我了解了创业教育领域的精彩之处，还给了我第一次教学的机会。

Beyond the obvious influence from Steve Blank and Eric Ries, a big thanks to some other writers who have directly helped this book with their work: Amy Hoy on worldviews, Brant Cooper on segmentation, Richard Rumelt and Lafley/Martin on strategy, Neil Rackham on sales, and Derek Sivers on remembering that businesses are meant to make you happy.

除了史蒂夫·布兰克和埃里克·里斯的巨大影响之外，还要特别感谢那些直接为本书的撰写做出贡献的作者们：艾米·霍伊在“世界观”方面提供了宝贵意见；布兰特·库珀则在“市场细分”方面给予了帮助；理查德·鲁梅尔特以及拉夫利/马丁则在“战略制定”方面提供了专业见解；尼尔·拉克姆则就“销售技巧”提出了宝贵建议。最后，德里克·西弗斯提醒我们：企业的存在目的，就是让顾客感到幸福。

And of course, big thanks for Mom & Dad for gently planting the entrepreneurial seed through both encouragement and their own collection of insane startup and/or shipwreck stories.

当然，还要特别感谢爸爸妈妈。他们通过鼓励，以及自己那些关于创业的有趣故事，为我播下了创业的种子。
